# PostPilot - new automation feature ideas (round 1)

Generated 2026-10-02 by a multi-agent run: 12 research lenses -> dedupe -> 3 independent skeptics per idea (novelty vs the code, real time saved, feasibility). Ideas only: nothing here is built yet. Score = honest minutes saved per developer per week / effort weight (S=1, M=2.5, L=5). A second round (gap critic) had not finished when this was saved.

**35 kept, 1 rejected, 18 dropped as duplicates or already present.**

## Ranked ideas that survived

### 1. HMAC signature auth type with provider presets (webhook sender)
- **Score 3.0** | effort S | ~3 min/week saved | persona backend | lenses: backend-daily | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** Backend devs building webhook receivers (Stripe, GitHub, Shopify, Slack, payment providers feeding Odoo) must send correctly signed test calls. In an API client that is a pre-request script: build the signed string, HMAC-SHA256 with the secret, hex/base64, header like t=...,v1=..., keep the timestamp fresh (10-20 min the first time, brittle). PostPilot has no scripts (cannot run on web), so these calls cannot be sent; only AWS SigV4 and JWT are signed natively.
- **What PostPilot would do:** New auth type 'HMAC signature': pick a preset (GitHub X-Hub-Signature-256, Stripe-Signature, Shopify, Slack, generic) or set algorithm, secret, header name, encoding and template. At send time PostPilot signs the final body (after variables resolve) with a fresh timestamp and sets the header, so the dev only edits the payload and clicks Send. Presets ship sample payloads.
- **Where it lives:** Auth tab type dropdown (one new entry next to AWS Signature v4 and JWT Bearer); lives in the request spec builder, so runner and CLI work too.
- **Competitors:** Evidence checked. All three cited URLs are real and say what the candidate claims. The Tyk community thread (community.tyk.io/t/testing-hmac-with-postman/7131) is a user whose hand-written Postman HMAC pre-request script does not work. The Camunda forum thread (forum.camunda.io/t/looking-for-postman-hmac-pre-request-script/45469) is a user asking for a ready Postman HMAC pre-request script. The Therefore help page (help.therefore.net/ar2026/webhooks_ar/wh_signature_ar.htm) gives Postman scripts for HMAC-SHA256 hex headers, including variants with a unix timestamp. Competitors: Postman's authorization docs (learning.postman.com/docs/sending-requests/authorization/authorization-types/) show no built-in HMAC or webhook-signature type. The fetch summary was incomplete (it listed only 5 types), so treat this as "none found" rather than proof. Bruno's auth docs (docs.usebruno.com/auth/overview) list OAuth 1/2, AWS SigV4, Basic, Bearer, Digest, NTLM and Akamai EdgeGrid, with no generic HMAC. So the market answer is a pre-request script, which is the pain the candidate describes and which PostPilot cannot run on web. I did not verify Insomnia or Hoppscotch because the web search budget ran out. Vendor-specific workarounds exist (for example Stripe CLI trigger/listen and GitHub's webhook redelivery), but they are per-vendor and do not let a developer edit and sign an arbitrary payload. This is a real gap, though a narrow one. The per-use saving of about 3 minutes is plausible, but 5 uses a week looks a little high outside teams actively building webhooks. The one-time setup saving (10-20 minutes) is the stronger argument.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible with no rewrite. Fits the existing auth pipeline exactly; works on desktop, web and mobile (pure Dart crypto, no dart:io).

WHY IT FITS (verified in code)
- RequestSpecBuilder.build() (lib/features/request_builder/domain/services/request_spec_builder.dart) is the single place auth is signed. It already has the resolved body bytes (_buildBody -> body.bytes) and calls _applyAuth(auth, headers, resolver, uri, body.bytes, method), exactly as AwsSigV4Signer does. Send, code snippet, collection runner and CLI (workspace_runner.dart builds via the same const RequestSpecBuilder) all go through it, so "runner and CLI work too" is true with no extra work.
- Variables and dynamic vars ({{$guid}}, {{$isoTimestamp}}) in the payload are resolved BEFORE signing, so the signature always covers the exact bytes sent. This is the real advantage over a pre-request script. DioApiClient passes the bytes through unchanged (dio _transformData handles Uint8List raw), so no re-encoding drift.
- crypto ^3.0.7 is already a dependency (jwt_signer.dart, aws_sigv4_signer.dart). Web-safe.
- Persistence needs no DB migration: requests_table.authType is a text column (stores AuthType.name) and the rest lives in authConfigJson; RequestAuth.fromJson tolerates missing keys. Collection auth (collection_auth_table authJson) works the same, so one "Webhooks" collection can set HMAC once and every request inherits it.

DESIGN
1. core/enums/auth_type.dart: add AuthType.hmacSignature (label 'HMAC Signature'), enums HmacAlgorithm (sha1 legacy, sha256, sha384, sha512), HmacEncoding (hex, base64, base64url), HmacSecretFormat (text UTF-8 | base64 with optional whsec_ prefix stripped | hex).
2. New pure service request_builder/domain/services/hmac_signer.dart, modelled on AwsSigV4Signer: sign({method, uri, body bytes, now}) returns Map<String,String> headers. Build the signed message as BYTES (utf8 template pieces + raw body bytes via BytesBuilder), never decode/re-encode the body.
3. Generalise the candidate's header model: instead of headerName/timestampHeader fields, use ONE multi-line field hmacHeaders ("Name: template" per line) plus hmacSignedTemplate. Placeholders (single braces, so they never collide with {{vars}}; AppConstants.variablePattern is \{\{[\w.$-]+\}\}): {timestamp} (unix s, generated once), {timestampMs}, {isoTimestamp}, {id} (uuid, generated once), {body}, {method}, {path}, {url}, {signature}. This single model covers GitHub (X-Hub-Signature-256: sha256={signature}), Stripe (Stripe-Signature: t={timestamp},v1={signature}; signed "{timestamp}.{body}"), Shopify (X-Shopify-Hmac-Sha256, base64), Slack (two headers: X-Slack-Signature v0=... and X-Slack-Request-Timestamp; signed "v0:{timestamp}:{body}") and Standard Webhooks/Svix (webhook-id, webhook-timestamp, webhook-signature "v1,{signature}"; signed "{id}.{timestamp}.{body}"; base64 secret). Substitute placeholders in ONE replaceAllMapped pass (not chained replaceAll) so a body containing the text "{timestamp}" is never re-substituted.
4. RequestAuth: add 7 flat fields (hmacPreset, hmacAlgorithm, hmacSecret, hmacSecretFormat, hmacEncoding, hmacSignedTemplate, hmacHeaders) and touch all FIVE places: ctor, copyWith, withOAuth2Token (copies every field by hand), toJson, fromJson. Stay flat, NOT a nested object, because SecretFields.stripAuth / SecretSplitter only look at top-level keys.
5. RequestSpecBuilder: add case AuthType.hmacSignature in _applyAuth; resolve secret/templates with resolver.resolve inside the case; remove any same-named existing header case-insensitively before adding (the current code just assigns headers['Authorization'], which can double a header written in another case). Add an injectable clock: const RequestSpecBuilder({this.clock = _utcNow}) with a top-level function default (const tear-off keeps every existing 'const RequestSpecBuilder()' call valid) so tests are deterministic. Throw InvalidRequestException('HMAC secret is empty ...') when the secret resolves to empty; this matters in CI where workspace.local.json is absent.
6. UI (auth_editor.dart): one new dropdown entry plus _hmacFields(): Preset dropdown, Secret (obscured VariableTextFormField), then a collapsed 'Advanced' ExpansionTile (algorithm, encoding, secret format, signed-string template, headers). Presets are a pure domain table (hmac_presets.dart) that SEED the explicit fields on selection, so saved requests do not depend on a preset lookup and presets can evolve. UI gotcha: VariableTextFormField uses initialValue, so after a preset change the fields will not refresh. Key the fields subtree by hmacPreset (as _OAuth2Fields does with ValueKey), and do NOT flip the preset to 'custom' on edit, otherwise every keystroke changes the key, rebuilds and drops focus. No ToolDialog needed for v1.
7. Mandatory secret plumbing: add 'hmacSecret' to SecretFields.authKeys (git_sync/domain/services/secret_fields.dart). That ONE set feeds Git-push stripping, DocValues._authMap local-secret restore, SecretSplitter (workspace.local.json for export/CLI) and the test fakes. Also add 'hmac' to SecretMasker._nameFragments (Shopify's X-Shopify-Hmac-Sha256 header is not caught today; X-Hub-Signature-256 / Stripe-Signature / X-Slack-Signature already match 'signature').
8. Exhaustive switches the compiler will force: auth_type.dart label, auth_editor.dart, postman_collection_exporter._authOf (Postman has no HMAC type: emit {'type':'noauth'} and never export the secret), openapi_exporter._schemeOf (register apiKey/header scheme named by the first header). test/git_sync/local_collection_store_test.dart authFor() is an exhaustive switch over AuthType.values and its rich-fixture round trip will then automatically cover the new type.
9. Samples: instead of touching the body editor from AuthEditor (it only has onChanged(RequestAuth)), add 3-4 requests with sample payloads to the existing httpbin starter template in templates/domain/starter_templates.dart (target https://httpbin.org/anything, which echoes the generated headers, so it works without an account); optional follow-up: a palette command 'HMAC: show string to sign' opening a ToolDialog that runs PrepareRequestUseCase and shows signed string + headers (debugging a mismatch is the real time sink; about +2 days).
10. Tests (follow jwt_signer_test.dart / aws_sigv4_signer_test.dart style): GitHub's documented vector (secret "It's a Secret to Everybody", body "Hello, World!" -> 757107ea0eb2509fc211221cce984b8a37570b6d7586c22c46f4379c8b043e17; I re-computed this with openssl); Stripe, Slack, Shopify, Standard Webhooks recomputed independently with openssl/python; fixed-clock builder test; variables/dynamic vars in body signed after resolution; empty-secret error; Git-sync strip + restore of hmacSecret; plus a GUARD test that every RequestAuth.toJson() key whose name contains secret/password/token is in SecretFields.authKeys (the existing test at local_collection_store_test.dart:564 only checks listed keys are stripped, it would not catch a forgotten one).

CORRECTED EFFORT: S, realistically 4-6 dev days for the header-only scope (signer 1d, RequestAuth plumbing + secrets 0.5d, UI with presets/advanced 1-1.5d, importers/exporters/docs 0.5d, vectors + git-sync + guard tests 1-1.5d). It becomes M only if scope grows to: signature in the QUERY string (Binance-style exchange APIs; _buildUrl runs before the body and auth, so the signed query must be assembled first), or the string-to-sign preview dialog, or auto-detecting signed cURL on paste.

PLATFORM: web, desktop, mobile all fine (crypto is pure Dart, DateTime.now().toUtc() works on web). Web keeps its pre-existing limits: CORS on the receiver and browser-forbidden headers; none of the preset headers is forbidden, but a receiver without CORS headers will still fail from a browser, same as every other request on web.
- **Risks:** LEGAL/ToS/STORE: low. HMAC-SHA1/256/384/512 over text is public, vendor-documented verification (GitHub, Stripe, Shopify, Slack, Standard Webhooks all publish it), no scraping and no vendor API terms involved. Use vendor names only as plain preset labels (nominative use), no logos. HMAC for authentication is not an App Store export-compliance encryption issue and the app already does TLS, SigV4, JWT HS*.

SECURITY (the main risk, mitigable):
1) Secret leakage into the Git-synced workspace if 'hmacSecret' is forgotten in SecretFields.authKeys. The existing tests do not catch a missing key, so add the guard test above. Because git push, workspace.json export and the CLI local-secrets file all share that set, one line plus the guard covers all of them.
2) Same plaintext-in-SQLite storage as jwtSecret and awsSecretKey today. Consistent, not worse. Encourage a secret-marked environment variable reference ({{stripe_whsec}}) in the preset hint; it resolves fine and is split out by SecretSplitter.
3) The generated signature headers are replayable within the receiver's tolerance (Stripe about 5 min) and end up in Console, code snippets and bug-report text. Masker already covers 'signature'; add 'hmac' for Shopify.
4) Empty secret silently produces a valid-looking but wrong signature (Dart's Hmac accepts an empty key), which is exactly the CI case where workspace.local.json is missing. Must throw InvalidRequestException.

CORRECTNESS TRAPS: sign bytes, not decoded text; single-pass placeholder substitution; timestamp and {id} generated once and reused in every header and in the signed string (Slack and Standard Webhooks need that, which is why a separate timestamp variable like {{$timestamp}} in a header row would drift); case-insensitive header override; fields subtree keying so presets refresh without losing focus on edit; RequestAuth.withOAuth2Token must copy the new fields or HMAC config is dropped when an OAuth token is stored.

MAINTENANCE: RequestAuth is already a 30-field flat class with 5 hand-written copy sites; this adds 7 more. Acceptable now, but it is growing debt (consider a refactor before the next auth type, not as a blocker).

PRODUCT HONESTY: the 3 min x 5/week figure is generous for a typical developer; most devs touch webhook signing in bursts (when writing a receiver), so 3 uses/week averaged over a year is closer to 1-2. The one-time setup saving (about 15 min per webhook) is the more credible number. Value is concentrated in backend and Odoo developers writing payment-provider or webhook controllers; the feature is still worth building because the cost is small and it fills a gap the no-scripting design otherwise leaves (it also works in runner, CLI and web)."
- **Strongest objection:** The 3 min x 5 uses/week figure is inflated, and the demand evidence is thin.

1. **Usage is bursty, not weekly.** A backend dev signs test webhooks heavily for a few days while building a receiver, then not at all for months. Averaged over a year that is about 1 use a week. Flutter devs almost never need it. Odoo devs only need it when writing a custom payment-provider module.

2. **Per-use saving is overstated.** Once a signing script exists, later sends cost nothing, so the real saving is the one-time setup. That is about 15 min per new integration, at roughly 6 new integrations a year, or about 1.7 min/week. The no-scripts case adds a manual `openssl dgst -hmac` step on each payload edit, about 1.5 min x roughly 40 sends a year in bursts, or about 1 min/week. That totals about 3 min/week for a backend dev and about 1 for the other personas.

3. **Vendors already solve the big presets.** `stripe listen --forward-to` and `stripe trigger` send correctly signed events. GitHub has a redeliver button for webhooks. Shopify has a CLI webhook trigger. Slack mostly goes through real app events. These are the Stripe, GitHub and Shopify presets this candidate leads with, and devs already use these tools. The remaining cases are custom or internal HMAC schemes, where the preset dropdown doesn't help and the dev would still pick algorithm, template and header by hand.

4. **Evidence is weak and mismatched.** I could only fetch two of the four cited sources; the other two are PostPilot's own source files. The Tyk thread (Feb 2024) is about HMAC request signing for Tyk's own auth, not webhooks. It has 2 replies and is unresolved. The Camunda thread (Jun 2023) is a webhook case, with 2 replies, and the poster self-resolved by finding a vendor blog post. Neither is from 2025-2026, and neither shows volume. The therefore.net page is just vendor docs pointing at a script. My web-search budget ran out, so I could not look for a Postman or Insomnia feature request with real vote counts.

**What would make it worth building.** The strongest framing is generic HMAC request signing for outbound API calls, such as exchange APIs, payment gateways and Tyk-style gateways, with webhooks as one use of it. It is a cheap S-effort gap-filler that rides on the existing `crypto` and `aws_sigv4_signer` pattern, so it is worth doing as hygiene. It is not a headline time-saver, and it should not displace features that cut minutes on a daily path.
- **Platform notes:** Uses the crypto package already in the project, so it works on web too. Secret stored in local-only secret fields, never in the Git-synced workspace. One-time setup per webhook saves another ~15 min on top of the per-use number.

### 2. Cleanup ledger: auto-delete the records a request, run or Odoo flow created
- **Score 2.8** | effort M | ~7 min/week saved | persona all | lenses: odoo-daily, data-flows | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** Create-flows (login -> create order -> verify) and data-driven runs leave junk on staging, local and Odoo databases, and there is no rollback: each Odoo JSON-2 call is its own committed transaction. Devs copy created ids out of responses, edit the unlink/DELETE request and send it once per record (3-10 min after a run), or leave junk and later hit unique-name/count failures. If an assertion fails mid-run the manual cleanup never executes; posted invoices/confirmed orders cannot just be unlinked.
- **What PostPilot would do:** A request gets one optional 'Cleanup request' row (the delete/unlink request; its {{vars}} map from this request's extractors). Whenever the request succeeds (send, runner, bulk run) a persisted 'Created records' ledger stores the variable snapshot. The runner footer shows 'Clean up 37 records' plus a toggle 'Clean up automatically after the run, even if tests failed'; a palette command cleans the last run. Cleanup runs the paired requests in reverse order. For Odoo it watches /json/2/<model>/create, remembers (model, ids) and uses per-model recipes (sale.order action_cancel then unlink, account.move button_draft then unlink); the report says 'created 12, deleted 11, left 1 (posted and locked)'. Created ids also become variables like {{created.res.partner.0}}.
- **Where it lives:** 'Cleanup' section at the bottom of the request Tests tab, footer action in the Collection Runner dialog, palette command 'Clean up created records'; CLI flag --odoo-cleanup.
- **Competitors:** I could not verify competitor claims at length because the web search budget was exhausted (200/200). One WebFetch of Postman's test-scripts docs found no built-in teardown or cleanup feature. Postman users write post-response scripts or add a final DELETE request by hand, and I did not see a Postman, Insomnia or Bruno feature that records created ids and deletes them automatically. This is not an exhaustive search, and it should not be read as proof that no tool has one. Karate has afterScenario hooks and Playwright documents creating in beforeAll and deleting in afterAll over the API, but both are hand-written code, not an automatic ledger. Evidence check: the Odoo 19 External JSON-2 page (https://www.odoo.com/documentation/19.0/developer/reference/external_api.html) does list a "Transaction" section. WebFetch returned only the table of contents, so I could not confirm the "own committed transaction per call" wording. It is consistent with the Odoo docs as I recall them. The Playwright and Odoo testing URLs in the candidate were not re-fetched. Design caveats: the candidate is new and not already built, but the scope is large for "M". The per-model Odoo recipes (cancel or draft, then unlink) are the risky part: they need a recipe table, blocked-state detection and an honest "left N" report. Cleanup runs as deletes, so it must go through the production lock, refuse on PROD unless confirmed, and never force. Pairing a generic "cleanup request" with {{vars}} is easy, since the VariableResolver layering already supports it. The Odoo create-watching ledger is the main value. A web-safe, pure-Dart implementation is feasible.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite, but only if re-scoped. As written (generic paired cleanup request + Odoo watcher + Git/workspace sync + CLI flag + {{created.*}} variables) it is L. The Odoo-first ledger below is M, and it is the part with the real payoff.

WHAT FITS THE EXISTING ARCHITECTURE
- Every send already passes one post-send choke point. RequestBuilderViewModel.send and CollectionRunnerService._sendOne both call RunRequestScriptsUseCase right after SendRequestUseCase, and both still hold the full response body there (the runner strips it afterwards in _withoutBody). So one hook covers single sends, the runner, bulk and data-driven runs.
- Cleanup can reuse the existing senders. For Odoo, OdooClient.call(OdooConnection, OdooCall) already returns a parsed OdooResult with OdooErrorParser output, and OdooJson2/OdooCall already build the unlink body. For paired HTTP cleanup, SendRequestUseCase(request, dataVariables: snapshot) works unchanged, because BuildVariableResolverUseCase already puts dataVariables on top of every other variable scope. History and the Console log therefore show each delete, which makes the cleanup auditable.
- The production lock is UI-layer (ProductionGuard.checkRun/checkSend plus confirmProductionSend). The cleanup dialog can call it exactly as collection_runner_dialog._run does, counting deletes as writes.
- ToolDialog, ToolTabs, StatusChip and BusyLabel cover the new dialog. The palette needs one PaletteItem in PaletteItems.tools(). The auto-clean toggle sits next to 'Stop on failure' in the runner's _Settings pane, with a mirror in SafetyPrefs/safety_settings_pane.
- DI is plain GetIt registration in core/di/injector.dart, so the new repository, use cases and ViewModel register like their neighbours.
- Web is fine. Everything goes through Dio/ApiClient, the DB through drift wasm, and the logic is pure Dart. The only limit is Odoo's own CORS, which already applies to every send. The CLI (WorkspaceRunner) is pure Dart with its own send typedef and no DB, so the detector and executor must be pure Dart and take an injected sender. Then a later 'postpilot run --cleanup' reuses them with an in-memory ledger.

RECOMMENDED SCOPE: PHASE 1 (M), ODOO-FIRST, NO PAIRING
New feature folder lib/features/cleanup/ (domain / data / presentation, same layout as odoo/ and scripting/):
1. domain/services/odoo_create_detector.dart (pure). Input is method + resolved URL + resolved X-Odoo-Database + response body. It matches POST .../json/2/<model>/create (also copy and name_create) on a 2xx and parses the id shapes int, [int] and [int, str]. It returns CreatedRecord(model, ids, baseUrl, db). Unit-testable with fixtures.
2. domain/services/cleanup_recipes.dart: a small data-driven map of model to ordered steps. The default is plain unlink. sale.order gets action_cancel with context disable_cancel_warning, then unlink. Each step is verified against Odoo source (see risks), and anything that still fails is reported as 'left behind: <Odoo error>', never forced.
3. domain/services/cleanup_executor.dart: takes entries newest first and reverse-orders them per run. Before unlink it re-checks the target (see safety below). It updates status pending/deleted/failed/skipped, honours cancel, and returns the report 'created 12, deleted 11, left 1'.
4. data: new drift table created_records (id, runId, seq, collectionId, requestId, kind, envName, hostKey, payloadJson, status, detail, createdAt, cleanedAt) in lib/core/database/tables/, plus a DAO and CleanupLedgerRepository(+Impl). Bump schemaVersion 4 to 5 with 'if (from < 5) await m.createTable(createdRecords)'. createTable is the path the existing migration note says works on web; addColumn crashes the web worker, so do NOT add columns to request_scripts. It needs build_runner to regenerate app_database.g.dart and the new DAO .g.dart. Add the table to clearWorkplaceData but NOT to workplaceDataChanges, because the ledger is local state and must never reach workspace.json or Git.
5. Hook: add an optional RecordCreatedRecordsUseCase dependency and an optional runId/request on RunRequestScriptsParams, and call it as the FIRST thing in RunRequestScriptsUseCase.call, before the 'scripts == null' early return. Odoo create requests from templatesFor() have no saved scripts, so recording after that return would miss almost every create. Optional dependency keeps existing tests compiling. The CollectionRunnerViewModel generates the runId in start() and passes it through run(); single sends use 'manual'.
6. presentation: CreatedRecordsDialog (ToolDialog, grouped by run, StatusChip per row, footer 'Clean up N' with BusyLabel, 'Forget' and 'Clear finished'). Entry points: palette command 'Clean up created records' (id cleanup.records), a conditional 'Clean up N records' button in the runner dialog actions (shown only when the run has pending entries), and the auto-clean toggle in runner _Settings (default OFF). Auto-clean runs in the VM's _onDone and stop(), so it fires even after failed tests or a Stop. Nothing is added to the request or response views.
7. Safety checks in the executor (required, not optional):
   - Every entry stores envName plus baseUrl and db. Cleanup refuses when the active environment resolves to a different host or db, and shows 'created on X, active is Y'.
   - Credentials are never stored. The API key is read from the active environment at cleanup time, and snapshots contain only non-secret keys.
   - On a PROD-named environment: manual cleanup goes through ProductionGuard.checkRun and the confirm dialog, and auto-clean is disabled and greyed out.
   - Entries older than about 24h need an explicit confirm.
   - Before unlink, run search [['id','in',ids],['create_date','>=',createdAt - margin]] and delete only the returned ids. Take createdAt from the create response's Date header, not the local clock. This protects against a staging DB that was restored or refreshed since the run, where id 57 is now someone else's record. Models without log_access fall back to the confirm.

PHASE 2 (separate M, optional): the generic 'Cleanup request' pairing
- This is where the as-written cost explodes. Persisting the pairing needs a new request_cleanups table, and it must be synced. The Git pull path deletes and re-inserts requests (see local_collection_store_impl and reopen_replaced_requests), so an FK-cascaded local-only pairing would vanish on pull.
- Syncing means touching BackupCodec (workspace.json and CLI), RequestDocMapper and local_collection_store_impl (a 'tests.cleanup' key with uid mapping), and the three-way-merge canonical form.
- The runner must also skip requests that are cleanup targets, otherwise the DELETE request runs as an ordinary step with unresolved {{id}}.
- The ledger snapshot comes from ScriptRunResult.extracted values plus the data row, since extractors overwrite the same variable every iteration. The cleanup request is sent through SendRequestUseCase with dataVariables: snapshot.
- UI is a 'Cleanup' section at the bottom of request_tests_tab.dart, which is a plain Column and easy to extend.
- Add the CLI flag only after the pairing is in workspace.json.

DROP FROM THE PITCH
- '{{created.res.partner.0}}' variables. Writing them into the environment would pollute workspace.json and show up as diff noise in the Git push preview. Extractors with path $[0] already do this explicitly.
- The hard-coded 'account.move button_draft then unlink' recipe (see risks).

CORRECTED NUMBERS
- Realistic payoff for one Odoo or QA developer: about 3-5 minutes per use, about 4-6 uses per week, mostly staging data-flow runs. The candidate's 4 x 6 is at the top of the credible range but acceptable.

TEST IMPACT
- New unit tests for the detector, the recipes and the executor (with a fake sender).
- A DB migration test for v4 to v5.
- di_wiring_test must include the new registrations.
- collection_runner_test stays untouched because the dependency is optional.
- **Risks:** DATA-LOSS RISK (the main one; acceptable only with the guards in the design).
1. Wrong target. Ids from a restored or refreshed staging DB, or an environment switched after the run, could point at someone else's records. Mitigations: store host + db + env name per entry and refuse on mismatch; apply a 24h age confirm; verify create_date against the create response's Date header before unlink; delete only ids the tool itself saw come back from a create call (never user-typed ids). Auto-clean defaults OFF and is disabled on PROD-named environments. Manual cleanup on PROD goes through ProductionGuard.
2. Odoo recipes are version- and company-specific and must not be advertised as 'works for invoices'. I checked Odoo 18 source:
   - account.move.unlink has two ondelete guards. _unlink_account_audit_trail_except_once_post raises 'To keep the audit trail, you can not delete journal entries once they have been posted' when posted_before and the company has check_account_audit_trail. _unlink_forbid_parts_of_chain blocks deleting a sequence-numbered move that is not last in the chain, unless the user is an account manager. So 'button_draft then unlink' only works for the last move in the chain on companies without audit trail. The honest fallback is button_draft + button_cancel and report 'cancelled, not deleted'. Never use the force_delete context key by default (it creates sequence gaps and defeats the audit trail).
   - sale.order is fine: _unlink_except_draft_or_cancel allows unlink only in draft or cancel, and action_cancel can return a wizard unless context disable_cancel_warning is set. I did not verify v19.
   - Records created as side effects (pickings, invoices, mail, stock moves from action_confirm) are not tracked, so a deleted SO can leave debris. Sent emails and webhooks cannot be undone. The report must say 'left behind' rather than 'cleaned'.
3. Unknown outcomes: a create that timed out or was cancelled with Stop may have committed but returns no ids, so the ledger cannot see it. Document this; do not claim completeness.
4. Verify against a real Odoo 19: I could not confirm from the docs that JSON-2 create returns a plain id list (the docs fetch only confirmed 'each call runs in its own SQL transaction, committed on success'). The detector should accept int, [int] and [int, str] and ignore anything else. Web search budget was exhausted, so Odoo 19-specific unlink behaviour is unverified.

ARCHITECTURE/EFFORT RISKS
- Hook placement: recording after the 'scripts == null' early return in RunRequestScriptsUseCase would silently miss most Odoo creates (templates have no scripts). It must run first.
- Schema: needs drift codegen (build_runner) and a v5 migration. Use createTable only; addColumn is known to crash the web drift worker in this project. Do not add a cleanup column to request_scripts.
- The generic pairing is the expensive part: it must sync through BackupCodec, the git doc mapper, local_collection_store_impl and the three-way merge, and Git pull re-inserts requests, so a local-only pairing would be lost. Also the paired DELETE request would be run by the collection runner unless excluded. Keep it out of phase 1.
- The ledger and its variable snapshots are local state. They must not enter workspace.json or Git. Only store variables the cleanup needs, never secret-named ones, and never the API key.
- ProductionGuard lives in the UI layer, so the executor needs an injected 'confirm' callback, and auto-clean after a run must not bypass it.
- Env-variable pollution (a {{created.*}} variable) would add noise to the Git push diff; skip it.

LEGAL / STORE-POLICY
- No ToS, store-policy or licensing issue. It makes the user's own authenticated API calls to the user's own servers, with no new permissions, no telemetry and no third party. The residual risk is user data loss, covered above. The UI should say what will be deleted before it deletes, and show a dry-run list first.
- **Strongest objection:** The claimed 24 min/week (4 min x 6 uses) is inflated, for four reasons.

1. PostPilot already covers most of the stated pain. In collection_run_options.dart, stopOnFailure defaults to false, so a failed assertion does not end the run. The runner also does one pass per iteration or data row, with extractors writing variables per pass. A DELETE or unlink request placed at the end of the folder, using the extracted {{id}}, therefore already runs after failed assertions and already runs once per data row. The "assertion fails mid-run so cleanup never executes" case only bites if the user turns on stop-on-failure. The remaining gap is small: an optional teardown flag so that request still runs when stop-on-failure aborts the run. That is an S-sized change, not a ledger plus recipe engine.

2. The 3-10 min manual cost is a one-time setup cost per flow, not a per-run cost. Once the teardown request exists, a rerun costs 0 minutes. A developer does not copy ids and edit an unlink request 6 times a week. They do it when they first build a create-flow, then reuse it. Ad-hoc single sends, where nothing is paired, are the main case the ledger adds. There the dev cleans up by hand only when the junk actually blocks them, for example a unique-name clash.

3. Odoo developers, the highest-value persona here, mostly work on throwaway local or dev databases. Their usual cleanup is dropdb or restore from a template, or `-i`/`-u` on a fresh DB, which takes seconds and is not a per-record chore. Odoo's own tests roll back inside the framework. Staging Odoo usually tolerates junk partners and orders. The Odoo auto-watch with per-model recipes is the only part with real new value, but it is brittle:
   - It only deletes directly created ids. Side-effect records (stock pickings and moves, invoices, mail messages, sequence gaps) block unlink or are left behind.
   - posted/locked account.move cannot be reset after hash or lock dates, so the headline "button_draft then unlink" recipe often fails exactly where it is needed.
   - Each recipe is a maintenance burden across Odoo versions and custom modules.
   Expect many reports of "left N". I could not verify the Odoo or Playwright evidence URLs: the web-search budget was exhausted, so I only read the code and reasoned from the candidate text.

4. Delete automation carries real risk. A wrong id mapping or a variable collision (for example {{id}} reused across extractors) deletes the wrong record. Even behind the PROD lock, auto-deleting on a shared staging DB is something a cautious dev will not switch on. Adoption of 'auto cleanup after run' will be low, and the per-request opt-in row adds setup friction that hits the same one-time cost as point 2.

Realistic estimate: for a typical dev, about 2 uses/week x about 3-4 min = roughly 7 min/week, mostly for devs who run create-flows on a shared staging DB. This is not a gimmick, but it does not clear the 10 min/week bar and it does not remove a chore that is both painful and error-prone for most users.

Cheaper alternative to build instead: a per-request 'Always run (teardown)' toggle so it still runs when stop-on-failure ends the run, plus an optional 'created {{id}} not deleted' warning in the run report. If an Odoo version is wanted, limit it to a one-click 'delete these ids' (model + ids shown, behind the PROD lock) rather than automatic recipes.
- **Platform notes:** Pure Dart, all platforms (web CORS permitting). Needs a persisted ledger table (survives restarts). Deletes data: run behind the production lock, refuse on PROD unless confirmed, report undeletable records, never force. Side effects such as sent emails/webhooks cannot be undone.

### 3. Self-renewing auth (auto-fetch/refresh OAuth2, re-login request on 401, also headless in runner/CLI)
- **Score 2.4** | effort M | ~6 min/week saved | persona all | lenses: competitors, pain-points, backend-daily, security-auth, data-flows | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** Tokens expire every 5-60 min. The developer opens the Auth tab and clicks Get/Refresh token, or re-sends the login request and re-copies the token, then re-sends the failed call (several times a day). A token that dies mid collection-run turns the rest into 401s. The CLI skips every OAuth2/Digest request ('needs the app; skipped'), so OAuth2 APIs cannot be tested in CI at all. Postman users fall back to hand-written pre-request scripts, which PostPilot scripting-lite cannot run.
- **What PostPilot would do:** One 'Auto-renew' section in the collection Auth dialog: either OAuth2 (fetch/refresh before send when missing or expired, with 30s skew) or 'Login request' mode (pick a request plus the variable its extractor fills; JWT exp or OAuth2 expiry triggers it). On a 401 it renews once and retries once, noting 'token renewed' in the Console. Same logic in the runner, CLI and MCP (client-credentials, password and refresh-token grants run headless). The developer clicks nothing after setup.
- **Where it lives:** Existing collection Auth dialog (new 'Auto-renew' section) and a small status chip in the Auth tab ('token valid 12 min'); palette command 'Renew auth now'. No new tab; runner and CLI just behave better.
- **Partly exists already:** PARTIAL: the manual building blocks exist, the automation does not. EXISTS (manual only): lib/features/request_builder/domain/services/oauth2_token_service.dart has fetchToken (client-credentials and password grants), refreshToken (refresh_token grant), Auth Code + PKCE (build URL, exchange pasted code), and static buildTokenRequest/parseTokenResponse. lib/features/request_builder/presentation/view_models/request_oauth2_view_model.dart and widgets/auth_editor.dart provide 'Get new access token', a 'Refresh token' IconButton, and a status line ('Token valid for 12m' / 'Token expired'). RequestAuth stores oauth2AccessToken/RefreshToken/TokenExpiry and has isOAuth2TokenExpiredAt; git_sync/secret_fields.dart already treats oauth2RefreshToken as a secret. The only callers of refreshToken()/fetchToken() are the two manual buttons in auth_editor.dart. MISSING, so the candidate's claims hold: (1) request_spec_builder.dart documents that the OAuth2 token is 'fetched ahead of time' and build() is synchronous, so it only copies the cached token into the header and never checks expiry or refreshes. (2) send_request_usecase.dart retries on 401 only for AuthType.digest (line 78); there is no OAuth2 or login-request retry. (3) cli/workspace_runner.dart line 231 skips digest and oauth2 with 'needs the app ... skipped', and cli/mcp_server.dart goes through that same runner, so CLI and MCP both skip OAuth2. (4) The in-app collection runner (collection_runner_service.dart) calls SendRequestUseCase, so it uses whatever token is cached and a token that dies mid-run gives 401s. (5) No 'login request' / auth-provider mode, no pre-request or setup-request concept, no 'Renew auth now' palette command, and no 'auto-renew' text anywhere in lib/, bin/ or README.md. The Auth section of README.md only lists Bearer/Basic/API Key/inheritance. Note: the existing token service is largely reusable, which makes the OAuth2-only slice cheap (S). CLI wiring needs adapting because the CLI uses its own CliRequest/CliResponse types rather than ApiClient. The 'login request' mode is the bigger new piece and is useful for non-OAuth JWT backends.
- **Competitors:** All cited evidence checked live and holds. Postman docs say it auto-refreshes OAuth2 tokens in the background before a send, but 'scheduled runs, monitors, the Postman CLI, and Newman don't support automatic token refresh' (learning.postman.com/docs/sending-requests/authorization/oauth-20/), so a headless auto-renew would beat Postman's CLI. Bruno has independent 'Auto-fetch' (fetch a token when none is valid) and 'Auto-refresh' (refresh on expiry) toggles (docs.usebruno.com/auth/oauth2-2.0/overview); Bruno issue #4392 is about resetting the cached OAuth2 token when switching environments (needs an app restart), a pitfall to avoid by keying tokens per environment. avilpage.com/2025/01/postman-auto-login-renew-oauth2-token.html is a 2025 write-up of the pre-request-script workaround for auto-login and token renewal. Insomnia also refreshes OAuth2 tokens (not fetched here, so not cited as evidence). PostPilot would match Bruno/Postman in the app and lead on CLI/CI and no-script 'login request' mode.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite. The seams already exist. I read the code only and did not re-verify the competitor URLs.

WHY IT FITS
- lib/features/request_builder/domain/services/oauth2_token_service.dart is already pure Dart. Its static buildTokenRequest and parseTokenResponse, plus fetchToken and refreshToken, cover client-credentials, password and refresh-token grants. It takes an ApiClient, which is a one-method interface (core/network/api_client.dart), and takes `now` as a parameter, so skew logic is unit-testable.
- SendRequestUseCase.call (request_builder/domain/usecases/send_request_usecase.dart) is the single place a request leaves the app. The request builder tab and CollectionRunnerService both go through it. Hooking it covers single sends and the in-app runner. Today it only retries on 401 for Digest.
- PrepareRequestUseCase re-reads collection auth JSON from CollectionAuthRepository on every call. After a renewal writes the new token back, re-prepare gives a fresh Authorization header.
- The production lock (ProductionGuard) runs in the UI layer (RequestBuilderViewModel.confirmSend and the runner dialog), not in SendRequestUseCase. An internal login or token request therefore never re-triggers the confirm dialog, which is the behaviour the candidate asked for.
- oauth2AccessToken, oauth2RefreshToken and oauth2TokenExpiry are already in SecretFields.authKeys (Git strips them) and SecretSplitter (they go to workspace.local.json). The `{{var}}` resolution of OAuth fields is currently private to RequestOAuth2ViewModel._resolved. Extract it so the view model, the new service and the CLI share it.
- The existing _TokenStatus row in auth_editor.dart already shows "Token valid for 12m", so the status chip is not new work.
- JwtDecoder (response_tools/domain/services/quick_decoder.dart) is pure Dart with expiresAt. It handles expiry detection for login-request mode.
- The CLI is pure Dart with an injectable CliSend. The runner skips oauth2 and digest at workspace_runner.dart:231. The MCP server calls runner.runRequest, so it inherits any CLI fix.

DESIGN
1. New feature folder lib/features/auth_renewal/ with domain plus one widget, and no data layer.
   - AuthRenewal value object, stored as one nested JSON key `renew` on RequestAuth: mode off|oauth2|loginRequest, loginRequestUid, tokenVariable, ttlSeconds. This avoids more flat fields. RequestAuth hand-copies every field in the constructor, copyWith, withOAuth2Token, toJson and fromJson, so add it in those 5 places. It is non-secret, so it syncs through Git and backup and tolerates old JSON.
   - AuthRenewalService (pure Dart, no Flutter imports, because the CLI shares it). It checks expiry with 30s skew, runs a single-flight Future per collection, keeps a failure circuit breaker, and handles the 401 path.
2. App integration.
   - Add an optional trailing constructor parameter to SendRequestUseCase, so test/send_request_usecase_test.dart still compiles.
   - Before prepare, call ensureFresh. After a 401, if renew is enabled, renew once, re-prepare and re-send once. Register in injector.dart `_registerRequestBuilder`.
   - Avoid a DI cycle: the service gets a `sendOnce` callback from SendRequestUseCase for the login request, instead of depending on it.
   - OAuth2 mode: OAuth2TokenService.refreshToken, falling back to fetchToken. Write the token back via CollectionAuthRepository.setAuthJson.
   - Login-request mode: look up the request via EntityUidsDao.localIdOf('request', uid), which is stable across Git restore (a numeric id is not). Send it with renewal disabled, run RunRequestScriptsUseCase so its extractor fills the variable, then re-prepare. Detect expiry via JwtDecoder.expiresAt, or ttlSeconds for opaque tokens.
3. UI placement.
   - An "Auto-renew" section in AuthEditor, shown only when allowInherit == false (the collection dialog), plus a Switch. The request picker uses RequestRepository.watchByCollection.
   - Optionally migrate CollectionAuthDialog from a plain Dialog to ToolDialog (icon vpn_key_outlined, 520 wide) to match the other tools.
   - Palette: add one PaletteItem 'auth.renew' ("Renew auth now"). Its run callback gets a BuildContext. Get the collection id from ShellViewModel.selectedRequestLocation().
   - The renewal call shows up as a normal token POST in the Console, because LoggingApiClient logs every call. The Console stores only url, headersCount and bodySize, never bodies, so no secret leaks. No special "token renewed" note is needed.
4. CLI and MCP.
   - Add a small CliApiClient implements ApiClient that wraps CliSend. It maps spec to CliRequest and the response back to ApiHttpResponse, using RunOptions timeout and verifySsl so --insecure applies to token calls.
   - In WorkspaceRunner.runRequest, replace the oauth2 skip with: resolve the OAuth fields with the shared resolver, then ensure a token. Cache it in RunState keyed by collection name, with expiry. Pass the renewed auth as inheritedAuth (or request.copyWith(auth:) for request-level auth) to RequestSpecBuilder.build.
   - Retry once on 401. Keep the skip for Digest. Skip PKCE unless a refresh token is present, with a clear message ("run once in the app, copy workspace.local.json").
   - In login-request mode the runner just calls runRequest on the referenced request with a recursion guard, because extractors already write into RunState.

CORRECTED EFFORT: M overall, about 2-3 weeks, not S for the first slice.
- OAuth2 only, with the app hook, UI toggle and CLI: about 1.5 weeks. The candidate's "OAuth2-only is S" is optimistic. It covers a RequestAuth migration, the shared field resolver, the service with single-flight and breaker, the SendRequestUseCase 401 retry, the CLI adapter, and the secret-handling change below.
- Login-request mode adds about 1 week: uid reference plus picker, loop guard, extractor wiring, and the JWT/TTL expiry check.
- Suggested order: (1) shared resolver plus CLI headless OAuth2, the highest value and CI-only gap; (2) app auto-renew for collection OAuth2; (3) login-request mode.
- Add tests with a fake ApiClient and a fake CliSend, and an injected clock for skew and expiry.

BLOCKER THE CANDIDATE MISSED (S, but required for CI)
SecretFields.stripAuth removes oauth2ClientSecret and oauth2Password wholesale, and SecretSplitter moves them to workspace.local.json even when the value is just `{{clientSecret}}`. In CI there is no local file, so the CLI would see a blank secret and POSTPILOT_VAR_clientSecret could never be used. Fix both to keep template-only values, using the existing SecretNames.isTemplateOnly or hasLiteralSecret, as _blankItem already does for headers. Add a test that literal secrets are still stripped.

PLATFORMS
- App: all platforms. The service uses only ApiClient, crypto and dart:convert.
- Web: DioApiClient works, but the token endpoint must allow CORS. A Basic Authorization header forces a preflight, which many IdPs reject. This is unavoidable; show a clear error. url_launcher for PKCE is unchanged.
- Mobile: fine.
- CLI and MCP: dart:io only through the existing sendWithDartIo. Keep every new file Flutter-free, because api_http_response.dart already promises this to the command-line build.
- **Risks:** 1. Stale write-back and refresh-token rotation (HIGH if ignored). Request-level OAuth2 auth lives in RequestBuilderViewModel's in-memory entity, and _update() saves the whole entity. A silent renewal written to the DB would be overwritten on the next edit with the old token. If the server rotated the refresh token, the stale one is dead and PKCE users are forced back to the browser. Mitigation: v1 auto-renew is collection-level only (CollectionAuthDialog loads fresh from the DB), or keep renewed tokens in a separate cache that is overlaid in PrepareRequestUseCase.
2. Cross-environment token reuse (the Bruno #4392 class). The token is stored once per collection, so switching dev to prod would send the dev token. Mitigation: store a fingerprint (hash of the resolved token URL, client id, scope, audience and username) with the token. Renew on mismatch. A persisted field or an in-memory map are both acceptable.
3. Concurrency. Tabs can send in parallel, and with rotating refresh tokens a double refresh invalidates one token. Mitigation: a single-flight Future per collection.
4. Retry loops and lockout. Without limits, a 500-request run with bad credentials fires 500 logins and may lock the account or trip anomaly detection (for example Auth0 on password grant). Mitigations: renew at most once per send, retry at most once; the login request is exempt from renewal (otherwise it re-enters via inherited auth and recurses infinitely); after a failed renewal, a roughly 60s breaker fails the rest fast with "auth renewal failed: <reason>".
5. Retrying a POST after 401. A 401 means the server did not process it, so a retry is safe. Re-prepare so the multipart boundary and headers are regenerated. Only retry on 401, never on 5xx or timeouts.
6. Secret handling. The SecretFields and SecretSplitter change (keep template-only values) is security-sensitive. Test that literal secrets are still stripped and `{{var}}` values are kept. The new `renew` block holds no secrets and may sync. Mark the login-extracted token variable as a secret environment variable so SecretSplitter keeps it local.
7. Production safety. Auto-renew silently sends a login or token POST to prod. The lock cannot catch it because it sits in the UI layer, and that is intended. Make it visible: the call shows in the Console, and the dialog text should say renewal never re-asks. Password-grant credentials are already stored in the local DB, so exposure is unchanged, not worse.
8. Legal, ToS and store policy: low. All credentials are the user's own, the service just does the POST the manual button already does, and no third-party secrets or loopback listener are added, so app-store policy is unaffected. Password (ROPC) grant is deprecated in OAuth 2.1 and RFC 9700, and PostPilot already supports it. Show a small hint and do not recommend it.
9. Odoo value is low. JSON-2 API keys do not expire. Legacy session-cookie flows often return HTTP 200 with a JSON error body when expired, so the 401-only retry will not trigger there. Login-request mode helps Odoo devs only if the user adds a body-contains trigger, which is out of scope. Do not market this to Odoo users.
10. Scope creep and UI. Do not add a new AuthType or new tabs. Keep it to the one Auto-renew section, a Switch and a request dropdown in the collection auth dialog, plus one palette command. Reference the login request by uid, not numeric id, or restore and Git pull will break the link. If it is not found, show a clear "login request not found" error instead of failing silently.
11. Web CORS. The token endpoint may block browser calls. The feature degrades to the current manual behaviour, with a clear message.
- **Strongest objection:** The claimed 1.5 min per use and 15 uses per week are inflated, and the CLI and runner gap is already covered by features PostPilot has.

1. The CLI/CI half can be done today. I checked the code. workspace_runner.dart runs extractors and chains the saved variables into later requests, and the CLI takes --var name=value, which beats every other variable source. A first "POST token endpoint" request with an extractor that saves access_token already makes the rest of an OAuth2 or JWT-login collection run headless. A shell line such as --var token=$(curl ...) does the same job. The only things missing are the convenience of reusing the OAuth2 form and the 'skipped' notice, and a one-time 5-minute setup is not a recurring saving.

2. 'Login request' mode duplicates an extractor. It is the same login request plus extractor that the runner already supports. Today the developer re-sends that login request, which is one click and about 5 seconds, because the extractor writes the variable. They do not re-copy the token by hand, so the 'manual re-copy' pain is overstated.

3. The per-use time is too high. Renewing in the Auth tab is a tab switch and a click, about 15-20 seconds. Noticing the 401 and re-sending the failed call adds perhaps 20 seconds. That is about 0.5 min, not 1.5.

4. 15 uses per week assumes a short-lived token every working day. Many dev environments issue 1-24 hour tokens or static keys, and Odoo API keys never expire. Averaged over the target personas, I would guess 8-12 expiry events per week for developers who do have short-lived tokens, and none for the rest. That works out to about 4-8 minutes per week.

5. A token dying mid collection run is rare, because runs finish in seconds to a minute. Retrying once on a 401 would be convenient, but neither Postman nor Bruno retries on 401 either. Both only auto-refresh before sending.

The valuable part is a small slice: when an OAuth2 token is missing or within about 30 seconds of expiry, fetch or refresh it before sending. That is parity with Postman, Insomnia and Bruno and removes a nag. It is not a time-saver, and it should not be pitched as an automation headline. The full auth-renewal system plus headless CLI work (effort M) is not worth building as a flagship. I could not run new web searches because the session's search budget was used up, so the demand evidence rests on the candidate's own links and on my reading of the code.
- **Platform notes:** Pure HTTP, works on all platforms (web needs CORS on the token endpoint). OAuth2-only slice is S and can ship first. Authorization-code+PKCE needs the browser once, then refresh token renews headless. Tokens stay in workspace.local.json, never in Git. Retry exactly once, only on 401 (safe for POST), never re-trigger the production confirm for the login request once it is marked as auth provider; short lockout after failed login. Odoo JSON-2 API keys do not expire, so Odoo-only users gain little.

### 4. Fetch all pages: detect pagination and merge results (also as runner option)
- **Score 2.4** | effort M | ~6 min/week saved | persona all | lenses: pain-points, backend-daily, flutter-daily | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** List endpoints (users, orders, Odoo search_read with limit/offset) return one page. To count, search, export CSV or build a model, the dev copies the next cursor/offset into the next request by hand 3-10 times, or writes a Postman setNextRequest script (10+ min). PostPilot has no pagination handling at all.
- **What PostPilot would do:** When a response looks paginated (page/per_page, offset/limit, cursor/next_cursor, a 'next' URL field, Link rel=next header, or an Odoo list that exactly fills the limit) the Table/Explore header shows 'Next page' and 'All pages (max 50)'. It loops with progress, delay and cancel, merges arrays into one result usable in Table, CSV, Tree and Dart model, and stops on a short page, missing next link or the cap (default 20 pages / 5000 rows). The detected pattern is remembered on the request and the runner/CLI can use it as a per-request option. Odoo: offset/limit on search_read with 'loaded 1,200 of 3,410' via search_count.
- **Where it lives:** Button in the header of the existing Response tools Table/Explore tabs; option in the runner row settings; CLI flag. Main response view unchanged.
- **Competitors:** Evidence check (the web search budget ran out, so competitor claims are only partly verified). Verified by fetch: Postman's setNextRequest docs (https://learning.postman.com/docs/running-collections/building-workflows/) say it works only in the Collection Runner, Postman CLI and Newman and has no effect on a single send. That page does NOT mention pagination, so the candidate's wording "Postman documents pagination as a setNextRequest script" is an overreach. Cite it only for "scripted loops are runner-only". The Postman community thread (81288) is about polling a task until it succeeds, not pagination, so it is weak evidence. Cite it as the same setTimeout + setNextRequest loop pattern, or drop it. The Odoo 19 external_api page exists, but the fetch returned only the table of contents (API, Transaction, Code Example, Migration from XML-RPC/JSON-RPC), so the limit/offset claim was not confirmed there. The repo's own odoo_rpc_converter.dart lists search_read(domain, fields, offset, limit, order) and search_count(domain, limit) as method signatures, which is stronger local evidence. I could not verify any mainstream GUI client (Postman, Insomnia, Bruno, Hoppscotch) that offers one-click "fetch all pages" with merged output. From general knowledge, none ship it natively, and users fall back to scripts (Postman setNextRequest, Bruno scripting) or integration tools (Zapier, n8n, Airbyte have built-in paginators). Treat the "no competitor has it" angle as plausible but unconfirmed, and add a stronger evidence URL before presenting it as proven. Pain claim: the 4 min x 4 uses/week estimate is reasonable for list-heavy backend and Odoo devs, and the Odoo search_read + search_count "loaded N of M" piece is the highest-value, lowest-risk slice.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite, effort M (about 2 weeks for everything, or phase 1 alone in 4-6 days). The existing architecture already carries most of it.

WHAT IN THE CODEBASE MAKES IT CHEAP
- Persistence needs no DB migration. Per-request settings live in RequestSettingEntries.settingsJson, which the table comment calls raw JSON "so the set can grow without schema changes". It already flows through BackupCodec ('settings' key, additive, v1 files still read) and so through the Git workspace JSON and CLI snapshot. Adding a `pagination` field to RequestSettings (lib/features/settings/domain/entities/request_settings.dart) gives "remembered on the request" for free. RequestSettings.fromJson drops unknown keys, so the field must be added there. Its withX() copy helpers are boilerplate and should be refactored to a single private copy.
- Sending: SendRequestUseCase(entity) already re-runs PrepareRequestUseCase on every call. That re-resolves variables, re-signs auth (AWS SigV4 and JWT depend on URL/body), reuses the OAuth2 cache, and retries Digest. So the pager only needs to produce a modified ApiRequestEntity per page (url, queryParams or JSON body via copyWith) and call the use case. RequestSpecBuilder._buildUrl already lets a query param override a same-named URL query entry, which makes offset/page/cursor injection trivial for GET. SendRequestUseCase.call already takes cancelToken and dataVariables.
- JsonPathResolver (scripting/domain/evaluator) is pure Dart and covers itemsPath, nextCursorPath and nextUrlPath. ResponseReader gives case-insensitive headers. JsonTable gives the table and CSV. downloadFile() in core/utils/file_download.dart already has io/web/stub conditional imports, so "Download CSV/JSON" of merged data works on every platform.

DESIGN
1. New feature folder lib/features/pagination/, pure Dart with no Flutter imports so the CLI can use it (request_settings.dart and backup_codec.dart are already pure).
   - domain/entities/pagination_config.dart: strategy (offsetLimit | pageNumber | cursorParam | nextUrlField | linkHeader), param names, location (query | jsonBody | graphqlVariables), itemsPath, nextPath, size, maxPages (default 20), maxRows (5000), maxBytes (about 32 MB), delay (default 250-1000 ms), toJson/fromJson using SettingsJson leniency.
   - domain/services/pagination_detector.dart: deterministic, request-first detection. Look at the request's own query/body keys (limit, per_page, pageSize, take, $top, offset, skip, page, cursor, after...) and then at the response (next/next_cursor/nextPageToken/links.next/pageInfo.endCursor, Link rel="next" header, total/count). Odoo case: a bare list whose length equals the body's `limit`. Always overridable in the UI (param name, items path).
   - domain/services/page_request_builder.dart: ApiRequestEntity + config + state returns the next ApiRequestEntity. It is never saved; it is only passed to send.
   - domain/services/paged_fetcher.dart: loop with cap (pages/rows/bytes), delay, cancel (ApiCancelToken), progress callback, stop on short/empty page, missing next, or repeated cursor (loop guard), 429/Retry-After handling, and partial result on error. It takes an injected `Future<PageResponse> Function(ApiRequestEntity)` so three drivers can share it: the Response tools VM (SendRequestUseCase), CollectionRunnerService, and the CLI WorkspaceRunner (build+send via CliSend). Merging keeps the first page's envelope and replaces the items array at itemsPath, producing a synthetic ApiResponseEntity.
2. UI (keeps the main request/response views untouched):
   - Response tools dialog: ResponseToolsViewModel gets a nullable `merged` ResponseToolsData plus a progress state. TableTab is a StatelessWidget reading the final viewModel.data, so Table, Explore and Schema are switched to a `viewModel.shown` getter (merged ?? data). Share, Ask AI, Compare and Timing keep the original page-1 data, because merged bodies are huge, the AI input is clipped at 12000 chars anyway, and compare/timing of a merged result would mislead.
   - A slim PagerBar widget under the existing Table/Explore header row. It renders only when a pattern is detected (otherwise nothing, so no clutter). It has 'Next page', 'All pages (max N)', a progress line, a Stop button, and a small 'Edit detection' popover. Use Wrap so it fits 320 px phone width inside ToolDialog.
   - DartModelPane reads initialJson once in a late final controller, so give it key: ValueKey(mergeRevision) to refresh it with merged JSON.
   - JsonTable.find defaults to maxRows 1000, which would silently cap the merged CSV, so pass the merged row cap through (Table keeps showing 200 rows).
   - Persisting and runner: a 'Pagination' section in RequestSettingsTab (per-request, auto-saved, travels with Git sync), plus a runner-dialog checkbox 'Fetch all pages for requests that have pagination set'. The runner has no per-row settings today (only run-wide CollectionRunOptions), so the candidate's 'runner row settings' placement does not exist and this replaces it. CollectionRunnerService needs RequestSettingsRepository injected (DI is in injector.dart and covered by test/di_wiring_test.dart). Scripts run on the merged synthetic response, and _withoutBody still drops it afterwards.
   - CLI: RunOptions.paginate/--paginate and --max-pages. WorkspaceRunner.runRequest wraps its build+send in the same PagedFetcher. BackupRequest already carries `settings`.
   - Palette: one command, 'Fetch all pages of the current response', opens Response tools on the Table tab.
   - Odoo: the 'loaded 1,200 of 3,410' line needs a search_count call. Derive it by swapping the last URL path segment to search_count and keeping only `domain` from the body. It is optional and about 1 day; skip it in phase 1.
3. Phasing: P1 detector + fetcher + Response tools PagerBar (offset/page/cursor/nextUrl/Link), 4-6 days. P2 persistence + settings section + runner + CLI, 3-4 days. P3 Odoo count + GraphQL Relay pageInfo/endCursor in graphqlVariables, 2-3 days.
4. Tests: the engine is pure Dart and testable with a fake sender (the style of test/collection_runner_test.dart and test/cli). Cover per-strategy fixtures, cap/stop-on-short-page, repeated-cursor guard, cancel, and `{{`-neutralization.

PLATFORMS: no conditional imports needed, because everything goes through ApiClient/Dio, which already works on web, mobile and desktop (ResponseType.stream is used already). Web caveats: CORS applies as for any send, and the browser hides the Link / X-Total-Count headers unless the server sends Access-Control-Expose-Headers. Body-based strategies still work, and the detector should say 'header not visible to the browser (CORS)' instead of failing silently. DioApiClient joins repeated headers with ', ', so Link parsing must tolerate commas inside <...> URLs.
- **Risks:** NO LEGAL, TOS OR STORE-POLICY BLOCKER. It is user-initiated sequential paging that equals the manual workflow, with caps, a delay and a Stop button. It adds no permissions and bypasses no bot protection. The user stays responsible for the target API's rate limits. Default delay and caps should be conservative, and 429 + Retry-After must stop the loop and keep the partial result.

REAL RISKS TO FIX IN DESIGN (the first three are correctness/security, not cosmetic):
1. The candidate's claim 'reads only, so the production lock is not triggered' is wrong. The lock lives in RequestBuilderPage._confirmSend (ProductionGuard.checkSend), not in SendRequestUseCase, so a Response-tools loop calling the use case directly bypasses it entirely. Odoo JSON-2 (POST /json/2/<model>/search_read) and GraphQL are POST, and ProductionDetector.changesData treats POST as a write. Fix: call ProductionGuard.checkSend once before the loop (one confirm, not one per page). The runner already has checkRun, so no per-page prompt there. The CLI has no guard today and none should be added per page.
2. Variable and secret leak via injected values. If the next cursor, offset or URL is put into the entity and sent through the use case, VariableResolver will expand any `{{name}}` it contains. A hostile or buggy API could return a cursor or next URL containing `{{apiKey}}` and get an environment secret echoed back to the host. Fix: neutralize `{` and `}` in injected values (percent-encode in URL/query, {/} inside JSON strings) and add a unit test.
3. Credential forwarding to a foreign host via a `next` URL or Link header. DioApiClient only strips Authorization/Cookie on redirects, not on a body-supplied URL, and the next URL would be sent with the full spec headers. Fix: accept a next URL only when scheme+host+port equal the original request's, otherwise stop with an explicit message ('next page points to another host'). Never follow it silently.
4. Non-idempotent POST: auto-paging a POST that merely contains a `page` or `offset` key could repeat a side effect. Restrict auto-detection to a param present in the request itself, require the single guard confirm on POST, and never auto-enable on create/write/unlink-style paths (Odoo methods other than search_read, search, read, search_count).
5. History spam: SendRequestUseCase records every send and History has no retention cap. 20-50 pages per use would flood it. Add an optional `recordHistory` flag (default true), or record one entry per fetch.
6. Stale-state mismatch: the Response tools dialog only gets requestId and the displayed response, and loads the saved request via findById. If the user edited the request or switched environment after sending, 'Next page' would continue a different request. Safest design: 'All pages' re-fetches from page 1 with the current request (one extra request), and 'Next page' is offered only for the most recent send. Passing a sent-request snapshot from response_viewer is possible but more plumbing.
7. Odoo body detection: the request body is raw text that may contain `{{var}}` (not valid JSON). Parse with jsonDecode and fall back to a narrow regex for "limit"/"offset"; if neither works, say 'cannot paginate: body is not plain JSON' instead of guessing.
8. Memory and size: per-page bodies still honor maxResponseBytes (a truncated page makes the JSON invalid, so stop and report it). Merged results need their own caps (rows, bytes). Hold merged data only in the dialog VM, and in the runner drop it after scripts run. Do not feed merged bodies to Share/AI (clipped at 12000 chars already, but huge bug-report text would be bad).
9. Detection false positives and negatives (a list that happens to equal the limit; a nested items path; HAL/JSON:API/Relay shapes). Mitigated by request-first detection, always-visible 'Edit detection', and showing what was detected ('offset+limit, items at data.records').
10. Maintenance cost: three drivers (Response tools, app runner, CLI) must stay in sync. Mitigated by one pure-Dart PagedFetcher with injected send, but the CLI WorkspaceRunner has its own build/send path and needs a small separate integration and test.
11. Timing and metadata honesty: the merged result keeps the first page's status, headers and duration, so label it 'merged from N pages' and keep Timing/Compare on page 1 to avoid misleading numbers.

Evidence note: web search budget was exhausted, and the Odoo docs fetch returned no body details. Odoo search_read offset/limit and the POST path are confirmed from the repo's own odoo_json2.dart templates, not from the docs page.
- **Strongest objection:** The claimed 4 min x 4 uses a week (16 min) is inflated. Four points:

1. Frequency. Most weekly work is debugging one endpoint, where page 1 is enough. The dev already knows their own API's pagination params and bumps page or offset by hand in about 10 seconds. A full fetch-all is needed only for counting, exporting or verifying every item, which is closer to 1-2 times a week. Many of those cases are quicker as a DB query, or a throwaway curl|jq or Python loop (about 2-3 min, once, then reused).
2. The Odoo case is the weakest part. The pitch cites search_read limit/offset, but in Odoo omitting limit returns all matching records, and search_count gives the total. A dev who wants everything just leaves limit out, so the 'loaded 1,200 of 3,410' feature solves little. I could not re-verify this against the Odoo docs because the web-search budget ran out. It rests on my knowledge of the ORM.
3. Auto-detection is brittle and costs more than 'M'. Page/per_page, offset/limit, cursor, a nested next URL, a Link header and 'list exactly fills limit' all need per-API overrides (param name, items JSON path, cursor path). Wrong guesses produce silently incomplete merges, which is worse than a manual loop the dev understands. Merging up to 5,000 rows into Table, Tree and Dart model also needs memory and UI work.
4. Overlap and a better design. PostPilot already has extractors, variables and a runner with CSV data iterations. The painful chore the pitch cites, a 10+ min setNextRequest script, happens once per collection, not weekly. A general runner feature, 'repeat this request, following {{next}} from an extractor until the field is empty, max N', would cover pagination, polling and job-status waits with no heuristics and no clutter in the response view. If anything is built, build that, plus a plain manual 'Next page' / 'Load more' button, instead of the auto-detect and merge UX.

Evidence caveats: the code check found no pagination logic in lib/. I could not run new web searches, so the claim that Postman, Bruno, Insomnia and Hoppscotch have no built-in auto-pagination is from prior knowledge, not re-verified. The cited Postman page documents setNextRequest in general and is not proof that developers do this weekly.
- **Platform notes:** Pure Dart, all platforms (web CORS applies). Hard page/item cap and a stop button; detection must be overridable (param name, JSON path of items). Reads only, so the production lock is not triggered; keep a rate-limit delay (default 1 s).

### 5. Flow controls without scripts: poll until, retry, run-if, always-run
- **Score 2.4** | effort M | ~6 min/week saved | persona all | lenses: data-flows, pain-points | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** Scripting-lite has no loop. Async endpoints (report export, payment/KYC status, import job, queued Odoo job) force manual send-wait-resend 5-10 times (about 20-30 s each), or a fixed runner delay that is either flaky or wasteful. There is no 'skip if', no per-step 'continue on failure' and no 'always run' for cleanup; Postman/Bruno solve this with scripts PostPilot users do not have.
- **What PostPilot would do:** A 'Flow' section on each request with plain rows, no code: Repeat until [assertion: status / JSON path equals/exists / body contains] every N s up to M tries or T seconds; Run only if [{{variable}} set/equals]; On failure: stop / continue / always run. Send gets 'Send until condition' with a live 'attempt 4/12' counter and Cancel. Runner, reports and CLI honour the same settings and show attempts per request.
- **Where it lives:** Collapsible 'Flow' section in the request Tests tab (beside Extractors/Assertions), an item in the Send dropdown, attempts column in runner results.
- **Competitors:** Hurl: per-entry retry and retry-interval, with a job-polling-until-COMPLETED example (verified at hurl.dev/docs/entry.html). Bruno: bru.runner.setNextRequest, skipRequest, stopExecution and bru.sleep, which its docs describe as a way to poll slow APIs; these are script-only (verified at docs.usebruno.com/testing/script/javascript-reference). Postman: polling needs setNextRequest plus a counter script (from the candidate's evidence; not re-fetched). Apidog: no-code test scenarios with if/for/foreach (from the candidate's evidence; not re-fetched). No fetched evidence of a no-code, row-based poll-until, run-if and always-run UI in a Postman-style client, so this is a real gap for non-scripting users, though Apidog partly covers it. A further web search failed because the search budget is used up.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite; effort M (about 1.5-2 weeks incl. tests), as the candidate says. Do NOT add the "Send until condition" dropdown item (see UI).

WHAT I VERIFIED IN CODE
- Three separate send paths exist and all need the loop: (1) RequestBuilderViewModel.send() (lib/features/request_builder/presentation/view_models/request_builder_view_model.dart): confirmSend once, then SendRequestUseCase, then RunRequestScriptsUseCase. (2) CollectionRunnerService._sendOne (request_builder/domain/services/collection_runner_service.dart), which already has an injectable _delay seam, a _pause(duration, cancelToken) helper that races ApiCancelToken.whenCancelled, and only a global stopOnFailure. (3) The CLI WorkspaceRunner.runRequest (features/cli/workspace_runner.dart, pure Dart, own CliSend, RequestOutcome already has a `skipped` field, used by the MCP server too). The CLI does not reuse the app runner, so the loop must live in a shared pure-Dart piece.
- Cancel and delay primitives exist (ApiCancelToken, Cancel button, _pause). Web safe: Future.delayed only, no dart:io.
- AssertionEvaluator.evaluate(response, [assertion], resolver) is pure Dart and already used by the CLI, so the "until" condition can reuse AssertionEntity (status / jsonPathEquals / jsonPathExists / bodyContains) and its editor row with zero new evaluator code.
- Prod lock: ProductionGuard is called once per Send (page-level _confirmSend) and once per run (checkRun counts writes per request). So per-attempt re-confirming does not happen by itself, and a GET poll is naturally silent. Gap: checkRun counts requests, not attempts.

DESIGN
1) Model + engine, pure Dart, no Flutter imports (so the CLI can use it): new lib/features/scripting/domain/flow/ with RequestFlow {until: AssertionEntity?, intervalSeconds, maxTries, maxSeconds, runIf {variable, op: isSet|equals|notEquals, value}, onFailure: inherit|stop|continue, alwaysRun: bool} + flow_json codec + FlowPoller (attempt callback, done predicate, delay fn, cancel token, onProgress(attempt,max)). Hard caps: interval 1-300 s, tries 2-100, total <= 10 min (same ceiling as CollectionRunOptions.maxDelay). Honour Retry-After on 429/503. Treat connection/timeout errors as a not-yet attempt (useful for "wait until the server is back after deploy"); InvalidUrl/InvalidRequest errors stop immediately.
2) Storage with NO schema change: put it under a `flow` key in the existing request_setting_entries.settingsJson (RequestSettings). Reasons: AppDatabase's own note says Migrator.addColumn crashes the drift web worker, so do not add a flowJson column to request_scripts; createTable is proven but needs build_runner + DAO + migration v5 + clearWorkplaceData + workplaceDataChanges + backup + git store, and codegen is a known pain (C: drive full). With settingsJson, workspace.json/Git sync (request_doc_mapper passes 'settings' through generically; local_collection_store_impl writes it raw), backup (BackupCodec uses RequestSettings.fromJson/toJson) and the CLI (BackupRequest.settings is already parsed) all get it nearly free. Must edit RequestSettings: field, fromJson/toJson, isEmpty, ==, hashCode and the four with* helpers (they rebuild the object field by field and would silently drop `flow`).
3) Orchestration: new SendWithFlowUseCase in request_builder/domain/usecases that wraps SendRequestUseCase + the until-check + RunRequestScriptsUseCase and returns {response, scripts, attempts, conditionMet, gaveUpReason}. Used by BOTH RequestBuilderViewModel.send() and CollectionRunnerService._sendOne so there is one implementation. Intermediate attempts evaluate only the until-assertion (AssertionEvaluator + BuildVariableResolverUseCase resolver); extractors and the full assertion list run ONCE on the final response (otherwise variables are rewritten every attempt). Add `recordHistory` to SendRequestUseCase.call and record only the final attempt, or 12 attempts push real entries out of the 200-row History list. The console log (LoggingApiClient) keeps every attempt; it is capped at 200, which is fine.
4) Interactive Send: if a request has an enabled `until`, Send simply polls. RequestBuilderViewModel gets pollAttempt / pollMax / nextAttemptIn; response is updated after each attempt so the user watches status change; the existing Cancel button in _UrlBar shows "Cancel 4/12". The URL bar is already crowded (method, URL, layout, code, Send), so no split button; clutter-free. Run-if and on-failure are ignored on a manual Send (the user pressed Send) and the Flow section says so.
5) Runner: CollectionRunnerService gets BuildVariableResolverUseCase (and a flow lookup via RequestSettingsRepository.get(requestId)) as constructor deps, so DI and test/collection_runner_test.dart constructors change. run-if false yields a CollectionRunResult with skipped reason (passed=true, like CLI skipped). Replace the early `return` on failure by a `stopped` flag that keeps scanning for alwaysRun requests. CollectionRunResult gets attempts/skipped/gaveUp; collection_run_report.dart (summary, JSON, CSV) and collection_runner_dialog.dart row get an "N tries, T s" chip and a skipped state. collection_runner_view_model._onDone derives stoppedOnFailure from results.last and results.length < planned: that breaks with alwaysRun/skips, so the service must report the stop reason explicitly.
6) CLI/MCP: WorkspaceRunner.runRequest wraps its `send` in the same FlowPoller; run() handles runIf/alwaysRun/bail override; RequestOutcome gets `attempts`; reporters.dart (console/junit/json) print attempts. MCP inherits it.
7) UI: a collapsible "Flow" group in RequestTestsTab (features/scripting/presentation/widgets/request_tests_tab.dart) under Extract variables, built from existing rows (make _AssertionRow public for the single until-condition; numeric fields like request_settings_tab's synced_text_field). No ToolDialog and no command-palette change needed (a palette command "Send once, ignore Flow" is an optional S extra). New RequestFlowViewModel registered in core/di/injector.dart (+ di_wiring_test).
8) STALE-WRITE FIX (required): RequestSettingsViewModel keeps its own copy and saves the whole RequestSettings, so a second VM editing `flow` in the same row would be overwritten (or overwrite).
