- **Competitors:** Hurl: --retry, --retry-interval, --repeat, --jobs (parallel workers, 1 = sequential), --delay confirmed at https://hurl.dev/docs/manual.html. Bruno: CORRECTION to the evidence list. The cited data-driven page does not say 'sequential only'; the `bru run` docs (https://docs.usebruno.com/bru-cli/runCollection) list --csv-file-path, --json-file-path, --iteration-count and --parallel (default sequential), so Bruno does have a parallel option, though no documented rate limit or retry/Retry-After. Postman Collection Runner (https://learning.postman.com/docs/collections/running-collections/intro-to-collection-runs/): iterations, a per-request delay and an iteration data file, plus stop-on-error and a few persistence toggles; the page documents no parallel mode and no automatic retry/rerun-failed, so the 'Postman is sequential with one fixed delay' claim holds. Hoppscotch CLI was not re-checked (search budget exhausted). Net: Hurl is the only one with retry plus parallelism plus delay, and none of the three offer response-sourced rows or an Odoo query source, which is where PostPilot can differ.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite, but only as a trimmed scope. The runner, data parser, report exporter, prod lock and CLI already exist. What is missing is scoping, a scheduler, out-of-order results and a few safety fixes.

WHAT EXISTS (read in code)
- collection_runner_service.dart: sequential loop, one fixed delay, CollectionRunOptions.maxIterations = 1000. Data rows reach the send and the extractors/assertions through the dataVariables scope (SendRequestUseCase, RunRequestScriptsUseCase, BuildVariableResolverUseCase). The scope can be reused as-is.
- run_data_parser.dart: pure Dart. CSV with delimiter detection (so a pasted spreadsheet already works as TSV) and JSON array of objects.
- collection_run_report.dart: JSON/CSV export plus formula-injection protection (_cell). Downloads already go through the conditional file_download_*.dart.
- ProductionGuard.checkRun, ApiCancelToken, PrepareRequestUseCase (can preview a row without sending) and OdooClient/OdooCall (search_read) are all in place.
- DioApiClient already tolerates parallel sends: _ConfiguredAdapter counts in-flight requests, and the cookie jar is shared.

DESIGN
1. New pure-Dart RunScheduler in lib/features/request_builder/domain/services/run_scheduler.dart. It is a worker pool with concurrency N, a token bucket for requests per second, retry with backoff that honours Retry-After, and a pause gate. It takes the injectable delay that CollectionRunnerService already has, so tests stay instant. The app service and the CLI both use it, so the retry logic is not written twice. WorkspaceRunner is a separate re-implementation that does not share the app service.
2. Extend CollectionRunOptions with concurrency, maxRps, retry policy and recordHistory. Add scoping to CollectionRunnerService.run: either requestIds or folderId. Today requestsIn() returns the whole collection, and there is no folder or single-request run. RequestSummaryEntity.folderId is enough to filter.
3. Add a recordHistory:false parameter to SendRequestUseCase.call. Without it, a 500-row run writes 500 history rows (the DAO has no cap).
4. Extend RunDataParser for an array of scalars ([1,2,3] becomes a single column, named from the JSON path's last segment or 'item'), with a higher cap of about 5000 to 10000.
5. UI: move the runner from its plain 720x640 Dialog to the shared ToolDialog, so it fills the screen on a phone. Add a source selector (Paste / From response / later Odoo query), concurrency and rps fields, a Pause/Stop strip, 'Retry failed rows' and 'Export CSV' (the current CSV has no data columns, so add them). Group results by iteration number in a map, because _onResult assumes in-order arrival.
6. Entry points: add 'Run for each...' to the request menu (currently Rename/Duplicate/Delete) and the folder menu in collections_sidebar.dart. Add 'Run for each item' to the selected-array action bar in explore_tab.dart (there the target request is picked from a searchable list). Add a command palette entry.
7. CLI: add --data <file>, --iteration-count, --concurrency, --rps and --retry to cli_main.dart (_valued set) and RunOptions. WorkspaceRunner.run needs an iteration loop with a per-row top variable scope. RunState.resolver() currently has none, but overrides can carry the row. RunDataParser is pure Dart, so the CLI can import it.
8. Descope for v1: no Drift 'resume' table. 'Retry failed rows' works in-session, and 'Export remaining rows as CSV' covers resuming across sessions (re-import it). No XLSX package, since TSV paste from Excel already works. No file_picker (it is not in pubspec) in v1; paste plus an optional picker later. The Odoo query source is a follow-up S: reuse OdooStudioViewModel.connection and OdooCall search_read with pagination.

CORRECTED EFFORT: M (1-2 weeks) for the trimmed scope: scheduler + tests ~2d, service/options/history flag ~2d, VM/dialog/ToolDialog ~3-4d, entry points ~1d, CLI ~2d. As proposed (persistent resume, XLSX, file_picker, Odoo source) it would be L.

PLATFORM: the app side needs no dart:io. Web keeps the CORS rules and a roughly 6-connection browser limit per host, so keep the default concurrency at 1 and cap it at 10. Mobile works, but a run stops when the OS suspends the app. The CLI is dart:io only, as it already is.

REALISTIC NUMBERS: bursty. About 10-15 min saved per backfill or QA data run, roughly 1 per week.

EVIDENCE: verified from the vendor docs: Hurl --jobs, --retry, --retry-interval, --repeat and --delay (hurl.dev/docs/manual.html). Postman's runner has a data file and a delay but no parallel mode or retry in its docs (learning.postman.com .../intro-to-collection-runs/). Bruno's runner supports CSV/JSON iterations and its docs mention no concurrency (docs.usebruno.com/testing/automate-test/data-driven-testing). Hoppscotch CLI has --iteration-data and --delay, with no concurrency documented (docs.hoppscotch.io/documentation/clients/cli/overview). Retry plus a rate cap plus resume in a GUI client is a real gap.
- **Risks:** 1. JSON-body breakage and injection. Variable substitution is raw: VariableResolver and RequestSpecBuilder never JSON-escape. A CSV value containing a double quote or newline therefore breaks a JSON body, or injects extra JSON keys into a write request. The same hole exists in the current runner. Mitigation: a required pre-flight that builds every row with PrepareRequestUseCase (no send), shows row 1 as a cURL preview, and flags rows whose JSON body no longer parses. Optionally add an escaping variable form such as {{json col}}.
2. Production lock undercounts. The dialog calls checkRun(writes, ...) where writes is the number of write-requests in the collection, not multiplied by iterations. A 500-row run would warn about '1 data-changing request'. It must pass writes x rows and say '500 POSTs to PROD'. Keep the single confirmation, as proposed. This is a small fix in collection_runner_dialog.dart.
3. Concurrency vs extractors. RunRequestScriptsUseCase writes extracted values into DB environment/global variables. Parallel runs would race on the same key. Mitigation: when concurrency > 1, evaluate assertions but do not persist extractors, or disable concurrency when the target requests have extractors.
4. Retries can duplicate writes. Retrying a POST after a 5xx or timeout may create a duplicate record. Default policy: retry 429, and 503 with Retry-After, for every method; retry other 5xx and timeouts only for idempotent methods, with an explicit opt-in for POST. Honour Retry-After capped at about 60 s, add jitter, and set a max-retries limit.
5. UI performance. The view model calls notifyListeners per result and rebuilds the full entries list and summary each time (O(n^2)). That is fine at 1000 rows but not at 10k with parallel results. Throttle notifications to about 100-200 ms and compute the summary incrementally before raising the cap.
6. Out-of-order results. Concurrency breaks CollectionRunnerViewModel._onResult (it assumes iterations arrive in order), stoppedOnFailure (uses results.length < _plannedRequests) and the existing runner tests. Stop-on-failure with in-flight requests must be defined (let the in-flight ones finish).
7. History flooding. Fixed by the recordHistory:false parameter.
8. Privacy. Exports will now carry data-row columns (possible PII or tokens). Pass them through _cell() for formula injection, and mask error text with SecretMasker as the service already does. Nothing is persisted, so there is no new at-rest data.
9. Legal, ToS and store policy: no store-policy issue (no new permissions or file picker, no remote code). Concurrency and rps against third-party SaaS may violate their rate-limit terms, and Odoo Online may throttle. Mitigation: default concurrency 1, hard cap 10, rps cap, plain copy that this is for the user's own APIs and not a load tester.
10. The CLI runner is a separate implementation (workspace_runner.dart), so the --data support and the scheduler wiring are duplicate effort. The shared RunScheduler limits the duplication. The CLI has no iteration support today.
11. OAuth2 token refresh under concurrency could stampede. Serialise the refresh or run the first request alone.
12. For Odoo, a single JSON-2 write/action with many ids is usually better than N calls. 'Run for each' mainly helps per-record methods and error attribution, so market it that way and do not overstate the Odoo savings.
- **Strongest objection:** I read collection_run_options.dart and collection_runner_service.dart. The proposal overstates what is missing and overstates how often it is used.

1. Most of it already exists. The runner takes pasted CSV/JSON rows (up to 1000) as per-iteration variables, has a delay, stopOnFailure, live results and export. A dev who needs to hit one endpoint for 200 ids can already paste a CSV and run it. The "throwaway collection plus hand-trimmed CSV" pain is real, but it is a rough edge on a working flow, not a missing capability. The genuinely new parts are concurrency, requests-per-second, Retry-After handling, resume or retry-failed-rows, a file picker, XLSX, an Odoo query source and CLI flags.

2. The usage frequency of 1.5 per week is too high. Mass backfills and mass fixes are rare for a typical developer, about once or twice a month. Daily work is single requests, collection runs for testing, and debugging. The people who really do bulk loads (data migration, load testing) already use scripts, k6, hey, Hurl --jobs or the Postman CLI. I could not run the demand searches because the web search budget was exhausted, so none of this is backed by evidence. My estimate is about 0.3 uses per week.

3. Odoo, one of the three target personas, is the weakest fit. Mass changes in Odoo are normally done with the Odoo shell, a server action, or the built-in CSV import. JSON-2 can also write to many ids in one call, so looping one request per record is the wrong pattern there. The "Odoo model + domain as a data source" idea would encourage a worse approach.

4. Concurrency plus many writes through a GUI client is risky, especially on prod. Partial failures leave an inconsistent state, and a prod mass fix is usually a reviewed script or SQL run in a transaction. The single "500 POSTs to PROD" confirmation does not fix that.

5. Honest saving per use. Over the existing runner, concurrency, rate limit and resume save about 5 to 10 minutes when needed. The 15-20 minute script comparison only holds for developers who would not just use the current runner. That gives 0.3 uses per week at about 8 to 10 minutes, so roughly 3 minutes per week. This is below the 10-minute bar.

6. The scope is too big for the value. M effort for a Drift table and migration, XLSX, file_picker, an Odoo source, a concurrency engine and CLI flags is poor value for a rare use.

What is worth keeping as a small S-sized change instead: (a) 'Run for each item' on a JSON array picked from the last response, which chains list-then-detail or list-then-patch without export and reformat; (b) Retry-After handling and a 'retry failed rows' button on the existing runner. Without the concurrency, XLSX, resume table and Odoo source, those could pass on the painful-chore criterion. The full bundle as proposed should not be built.
- **Platform notes:** Reuses SendRequestUseCase(dataVariables:), RunDataParser, ApiCancelToken and run export; resume state needs a new Drift table + migration. File picking needs file_picker, XLSX needs the pure-Dart 'excel' package (only csv today). On web, ~6 parallel connections per host and CORS apply; keep default concurrency 1. Raw SQL/DB sources deliberately excluded; Odoo query source covers Odoo.

### 33. Odoo access doctor: explain an AccessError or empty list in one click
- **Score 0.8** | effort M | ~2 min/week saved | persona odoo | lenses: pain-points, odoo-daily | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** A JSON-2 call returns AccessError/403, a search_read returns fewer rows than expected, or a multi-company error appears. The cause is one of four layers: model ACL (ir.model.access), record rule (ir.rule), field-level groups, or the user's company_ids/allowed_company_ids. The dev enables developer mode, checks the API user's groups, ACL rows and rules (about 7 manual steps), impersonates the user and retries. PostPilot's error banner only says 'check access rights and record rules'.
- **What PostPilot would do:** A 'Diagnose access' button on the Odoo error banner and an 'Access' tab in Odoo Studio runs a read-only battery as the API key's user: has_access for read/write/create/unlink (model level), has_access on the failing ids (record rules), search_count per company with allowed_company_ids, own groups/company_ids, readonly/group-restricted fields; with an admin key it also lists matching ir.model.access rows and ir.rule domains. Output is a plain verdict such as 'ACL allows read; a record rule hides 41 of 120 records (company 2, user only has company 1)' with the fix (group to add, ready ir.model.access.csv row, or allowed_company_ids). Matrix copyable as Markdown for the client's Odoo admin.
- **Where it lives:** Button inside the existing Odoo error banner, a new tab in Odoo Studio, palette command 'Odoo: diagnose access for current request'.
- **Design in this codebase:** Feasible with the existing architecture, no rewrite. Verdict: build it, but scope v1 to what ONE key can really prove.

Where it lives (all under lib/features/odoo, following the existing domain/data/presentation split):
- domain/services/odoo_access_doctor.dart: pure Dart. Takes a caller function `Future<OdooResult> Function(OdooCall)` (so test/odoo can drive it with a fake, like odoo_logic_test.dart does) and returns an OdooAccessReport (domain/entities/odoo_access_report.dart: a list of layer findings: model ACL, record-level, company, field, identity; each ok/denied/unknown plus a fix sentence; toMarkdown() is a pure function).
- presentation/view_models/odoo_access_view_model.dart: a NEW view model, registered as a factory in _registerDevTools next to OdooStudioViewModel. Do not grow OdooStudioViewModel (already 214 lines).
- presentation/widgets/odoo_access_tab.dart: 6th ToolTab ('Access') in OdooStudioDialog (initialTab 5), built from ToolSection/InfoBanner/BusyLabel. Use a ListView of finding rows, not a DataTable, so it works on phone width (ToolDialog already goes full-screen under 600 px).
- Reuse as is: OdooClient (dio-based, no dart:io, so web/mobile work whenever Odoo allows CORS; the existing view model already has the CORS message), OdooCall/OdooJson2, OdooErrorParser, OdooModelInfo (to tell whether a failing field is in the user's fields_get), OdooConnection.

Three small edits outside the new files:
1. OdooClient.call gets an optional cancelToken (ApiRequestSpec already supports it) so a 20-call battery can be aborted; it currently has none.
2. Connection source: PrepareRequestUseCase is NOT in the DI container (SendRequestUseCase and GenerateCodeSnippetUseCase each construct their own). Register it as a lazy singleton with the same deps. The doctor then derives the connection from the exact request that failed: resolved URL up to /json/2/, `Authorization: bearer <key>`, `X-Odoo-Database`, plus model and ids from the path and body. This also covers a key pasted into a header by smart-paste cURL or set in the Auth tab, and fixes the problem that the banner only receives an ApiResponseEntity. Fallback: the existing loadFromActiveEnvironment().
3. Entry points: request_builder_page.dart line 459 passes `vm.request` to OdooErrorBanner (new optional param) which shows a 'Diagnose access' TextButton when the exception is AccessError/Forbidden and opens OdooStudioDialog on the Access tab with the request. Palette: one PaletteItem 'odoo.access' in PaletteItems.tools(); run() reads ShellViewModel.selectedRequestId and RequestRepository.findById. The 'empty list / fewer rows' case has no error, so the palette command is the only way in for it.

Probes, all read-only and run serially (cap about 25 calls):
- Model ACL: ir.model.access/check(model, mode, raise_exception:false) for read/write/create/unlink. I confirmed from the 19.0 source summary that check is @api.model and public. has_access on the model is the alternative; its public callability over JSON-2 I could NOT confirm (docs and source fetches did not return it, web-search budget is exhausted), so spike it.
- Which group to add: ir.model.access/group_names_with_access(model_name, access_mode) is a public @api.model method returning group names, so the 'add group X' fix works WITHOUT an admin key. This is stronger than the candidate assumed.
- Record level: has_access/check_access on the ids from the failing request body (recordset method, `ids` in body).
- Company layer: res.company/search_read (the standard employee rule limits it to the user's own companies; verify on a real DB), then search_count once per company with context.allowed_company_ids=[c]. Never pass a company outside that list (Odoo raises AccessError for it).
- Field layer: fields_get already omits group-restricted fields for the calling user, so if the field named in a field-level AccessError is missing from the user's fields_get, it is group-restricted. Parse the field name from the error message.

Corrections to the candidate's claims:
- 'Record rule hides 41 of 120 records' needs a second, privileged identity (admin key) because JSON-2 has no sudo and search_count as the same user only returns visible rows. Make the admin key an optional field that lives in the view model only (never persisted, never synced). Single-key mode must say 'N visible per company' and never claim hidden counts. That optional compare mode is +2-3 days.
- ir.rule._compute_domain is underscore-private (confirmed from ir_rule.py 19.0), so the effective rule domain cannot be fetched over JSON-2. With an admin key, list ir.rule rows (name, groups, domain_force) and show domain_force as raw text. python_literal.dart only parses literals, not expressions like company_ids or user.id, so do not try to evaluate rules; derive verdicts from probe behaviour instead.
- 'Own groups/company_ids': there is no whoami in JSON-2, and the existing connection test (res.users.context_get) returns only lang/tz. Identify the user via res.company/search_read and group_names_with_access. Add an optional 'login' field only if a spike shows a way to resolve the uid.

Must-do spike before committing (half a day): on a real Odoo 19 DB with a restricted user and 2 companies, check has_access/check_access over JSON-2, the exact AccessError JSON and HTTP status, whether non-admin users can read res.company, ir.model.access and ir.rule, and what JSON-2 returns for has_access on ids hidden by a rule.

Tests: pure doctor tests in test/odoo with a fake caller (one fixture per layer), plus a test asserting every method the doctor can emit is in a hard-coded read-only allowlist.
- **Risks:** 1. Unverified core premise: I could not confirm that has_access/check_access are callable over JSON-2 on 19.x (the docs and source pages I could fetch did not show it, and the web-search budget ran out). Mitigation: ir.model.access.check and group_names_with_access are confirmed public @api.model methods and cover the model-ACL layer alone. Fall back to a search_count/limit-1 probe for the record layer. Run the spike first.
2. Over-claiming risk in the verdict text. Hidden-record counts, 'the user's groups' and an effective rule domain are not obtainable with one non-admin key. The UI must label such parts 'needs an admin key' instead of guessing. Wrong advice about security is worse than no advice.
3. Secrets: the probes send the user's real API key, so the optional admin key must be memory-only (it must never go through keepSecretsLocal or Git sync, and never into a saved environment). The Markdown export must contain no key, no row data and no PII; only model names, ids, group names and rule names/domains. Note that ConsoleLog does not mask Authorization (I found no masking in features/console), so the battery's 20 calls will land in the Console list with the key visible, as existing Studio calls already do. Consider tagging or hiding these probe calls.
4. Safety bypass: OdooClient talks to ApiClient directly, so the ProductionGuard confirm dialog is not involved. That is fine only because every probe is read-only. Enforce this structurally (the allowlist test) and show the active environment's PROD pill or name in the Access tab so a run against production is deliberate.
5. Load/rate limits: about 25 serial calls per run against a production Odoo (Odoo Online/Odoo.sh may throttle). Cap the calls, run serially, support cancel. Odoo Online plans without External API access return 403 for everything; detect that case and say so instead of reporting 'no ACL'.
6. Platform: no dart:io needed, so no conditional imports. Web works only if the Odoo server sends CORS headers, which stock Odoo JSON-2 does not, so desktop is the real target. This is the same limit as Odoo Studio today, so show the existing CORS hint.
7. Legal/ToS/store policy: none specific. It is normal authenticated API use against the user's own server, with no new permissions and no third-party service. Only Odoo 19+ is supported (JSON-2), which matches the stated target.
8. Value realism: 1.5 uses/week x 12 min is plausible only during integration setup and for multi-company projects; for a steady-state Odoo developer it is likely lower. The single-key v1 (ACL layer + group to add + company counts + field-visibility deduction) is the honest 12-minute saver; the admin compare mode is the part that gets the '41 of 120' headline.
- **Strongest objection:** The 12 min x 1.5/week (about 18 min/week) claim does not hold up. Frequency is inflated. AccessError comes in bursts when a new integration user or a new model is set up. In steady state a dev hits it roughly once every 2-3 weeks, not 1.5 times a week. Time per use is inflated too. Odoo 17+ AccessError text already names the groups allowed for a model ACL failure, and the fix is then about 1-2 minutes (add the group in Settings > Users). The 7-step cybrosys/deploymonkey guides are written for functional consultants working inside the Odoo UI, not for API developers, so they do not show that this audience wants the feature. A realistic average is about 4 min x 0.5 uses/week = 2 min/week.

The headline output cannot be produced by the proposed design. The example verdict "a record rule hides 41 of 120 records" needs to see records the key's user cannot see. A restricted key only returns its own visible rows. An admin key can count more rows, but an HTTP client cannot impersonate the API user (no with_user), so the admin's count does not show what the integration user sees. Listing ir.rule and ir.model.access needs base.group_system, so the probe is useless in the very case that hurts, a client's production Odoo where the dev has only the integration user's key. The ir.rule domain_force values are Python expressions (user.company_ids, company_ids, etc.), so a plain-English verdict means reimplementing Odoo's rule evaluation. That is a large and fragile build, not "M".

The most painful case, the silent empty list from a record rule or multi-company filter, raises no error. So the proposed button on the error banner never appears there, and only the palette command would cover it.

Today the dev does this faster. They read Odoo's own error message. In dev mode on a dev DB they open Settings > Technical > Access Rights / Record Rules. If they have shell access they run one line, `env[m].with_user(u).has_access('read')`. For multi-company they add `context: {allowed_company_ids: [...]}`, which is the common gotcha.

What survives is a small S-sized version. It would parse the groups out of the AccessError message in odoo_error_parser.dart (the current hint at line 105 is generic). It would add a has_access read/write/create/unlink probe per model, with ids [] for ACL only, and a one-click "retry with allowed_company_ids" for the multi-company case. That is a good banner improvement and a pre-flight check for a new integration user, not a weekly time-saver. The M-sized feature that was pitched should not be built.
- **Platform notes:** JSON-2 HTTP only (Odoo 19 target); web needs CORS on Odoo. Verify has_access is callable over JSON-2 on the target 19.x build and fall back to a read probe (search_count/limit 1). Listing ir.rule/groups needs an admin-level key: show a partial matrix otherwise. All calls read-only.

### 34. CORS doctor plus security-header check for Flutter web and API reviews
- **Score 0.8** | effort M | ~2 min/week saved | persona flutter | lenses: security-auth | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** A Flutter web app that works on Android fails in Chrome with an opaque 'XMLHttpRequest error' / 'blocked by CORS policy'. The dev cannot see which part failed (preflight status, Allow-Origin, Allow-Headers for Authorization, Allow-Methods, credentials with wildcard), tries random server tweaks and redeploys (10-30 min per incident). The same dev or QA also pastes URLs into securityheaders.com to check HSTS, nosniff, cookie flags and leaked Server headers.
- **What PostPilot would do:** A 'Security' tab in Response tools: 'Check CORS' sends the real preflight (OPTIONS with Origin, Access-Control-Request-Method/Headers from the current request) for an Origin the dev picks (default http://localhost:PORT), explains in plain words which header is missing and prints a copy-paste fix for Express, FastAPI, Spring, nginx and Odoo-behind-nginx. The tab also grades the response already received: HSTS, X-Content-Type-Options, CSP, Referrer-Policy, Set-Cookie Secure/HttpOnly/SameSite, version leaks, RateLimit-*/Retry-After.
- **Where it lives:** One new opt-in 'Security' tab in the existing Response tools dialog; palette command 'Check CORS for this request'. Nothing in the main response view.
- **Partly exists already:** The core feature does not exist: no preflight sender, no Origin simulation, no CORS diagnosis or server-fix snippets, and no security-header grading anywhere in lib/, bin/ or README.md. Only weak adjacent pieces exist. (1) lib/core/errors/unreachable_message.dart returns a one-sentence text on web saying an unreachable error may be CORS, with no diagnosis. lib/features/odoo/presentation/view_models/odoo_studio_view_model.dart:84 has a similar one-line hint for Odoo on web. (2) The Response tools dialog (lib/features/response_tools/presentation/widgets/response_tools_dialog.dart) has tabs Explore, Table, Decode, Compare, Timing, Schema, Dart model, Ask AI and Share. There is no Security tab. (3) The response Headers tab (lib/features/request_builder/presentation/widgets/response_viewer.dart, _buildHeadersTab) is a plain selectable list with no analysis. (4) A user can hand-write headerExists and headerEquals assertions (lib/features/scripting/domain/entities/assertion_entity.dart and evaluator/assertion_evaluator.dart), but there is no ready-made header audit. (5) The mock server (lib/features/mock_server/data/mock_server_engine_io.dart, lines 71-135) serves CORS and answers OPTIONS. That is the server side and unrelated to diagnosing someone else's server. (6) The Cookies dialog (lib/features/cookies/presentation/widgets/cookies_dialog.dart) only shows the Secure and HttpOnly flags of stored cookies. It does not show SameSite and does not grade anything. (7) lib/features/response_tools/data/network_probe_io.dart does a DNS, TCP and TLS timing probe only. (8) The Ask AI tab can read response headers (ai_tasks.dart) and could talk about CORS generally, but it needs the user's Anthropic key and cannot send a preflight. Verdict: roughly 90% new. The web-only hint is not a meaningful overlap.
- **Competitors:** I could not verify competitor claims because the WebSearch budget was used up (200 of 200). I have no confirmed example of Postman, Insomnia or Bruno shipping a built-in CORS doctor. Postman and others let a user type an Origin header by hand, but this is unverified here. From general knowledge, not checked in this session, the pain is usually solved with separate sites: test-cors.org for CORS and securityheaders.com for security headers. The MDN HTTP Observatory page checks HSTS, CSP, X-Content-Type-Options, Referrer-Policy, cookie flags and CORS, so the header-grading half already exists as a web tool. The evidence URLs in the candidate are real and reachable. I fetched all four: the dev.to Flutter web CORS fix article, the MDN HTTP Observatory launch post, the OWASP API Security Top 10 2023 page that lists API8:2023 Security Misconfiguration, and the corsfix.com blog post. Two cautions. The corsfix post mostly promotes its own CORS proxy and does not discuss preflight with Authorization or Content-Type headers, so that claim in the candidate is overstated. The OWASP link is the Top 10 index, not the API8 page itself. Honest value check: usesPerWeek of 1 and 10 minutes per use is plausible for a Flutter web developer but is low for the average developer. The header-grading half partly duplicates free web tools. The CORS doctor half is the real differentiator, because it works from the exact request in the tab, including its method, headers and Authorization. The web-build limitation is correctly noted in the candidate. Suggested reshaping: lead with the CORS doctor, make the security-header grade a small secondary section, and drop the five-framework fix snippets down to the two or three most common.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite, but it is M (about 1-1.5 weeks), not S. Reasons below. Facts are from reading lib/. The web search budget was exhausted, so I could not verify any Odoo 19 CORS behaviour or add new URLs.

WHY IT FITS THE ARCHITECTURE
- ToolDialog/ToolTabs, InfoBanner and BusyLabel are the shared kit. ResponseToolsDialog is just a list of ToolTab entries, so a tab is one line plus a widget.
- TimingTab is a direct template. It resolves the request through the view model, shows a "browser cannot do this" InfoBanner, and uses a conditional-import probe (network_probe.dart with _io and _stub).
- Palette commands are one PaletteItem line in PaletteItems.tools().
- Domain code stays pure Dart. The CLI shares ApiHttpResponse and ApiRequestSpec, so the analyzers can be reused by the CLI and MCP server later (not required for v1).
- A new feature folder lib/features/security_check/ with domain/services, presentation/view_models and presentation/widgets, as the other features do.

DESIGN
1. Domain (pure Dart, no Flutter import):
   - cors_preflight_builder.dart builds the ApiRequestSpec: OPTIONS, Origin, Access-Control-Request-Method, Access-Control-Request-Headers, Sec-Fetch-Mode: cors, Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site, Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty. The header list is lowercased and sorted, minus CORS-safelisted headers. content-type counts only when its value is not form, multipart or text/plain, and authorization always counts.
   - If the request is a "simple request" (GET, HEAD or POST with only safelisted headers), browsers send no preflight. The tool should say so and check the actual response's Allow-Origin instead.
   - cors_analyzer.dart takes the preflight response and returns a list of Finding(severity, title, plainWords, fixKey). Rules:
     - status must be 2xx, and a 3xx on a preflight is a failure.
     - Allow-Origin must match the origin or be '*'.
     - '*' together with credentials is invalid.
     - Allow-Credentials must be exactly 'true'.
     - Allow-Methods must contain the method.
     - Allow-Headers must cover every requested header. '*' does not cover Authorization and is literal when credentials are used.
     - Allow-Origin must have a single value. Dio joins repeated headers with ', ' (dio_api_client.dart line 108), so a doubled value like 'https://a, https://a' is detectable. This is a common nginx-plus-app mistake.
     - Vary: Origin when the server reflects the origin.
     - Max-Age.
   - security_header_grader.dart takes ApiResponseEntity.headers and is context-aware. Flag HSTS only on https. Flag CSP and X-Frame-Options only for HTML, never for a JSON API, or the tab fills with false failures. Also check nosniff, Referrer-Policy, Cache-Control, Server and X-Powered-By version leaks, and RateLimit-*/Retry-After parsing.
   - Set-Cookie arrives as one string joined with ', ' because the response headers are a Map<String,String>. Split with a heuristic: split on ', ' followed by a token and '='. The Expires date ('Wed, 21 Oct...') does not match that, so it survives. Then read Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite. Test this with Expires cookies.
   - cors_fix_snippets.dart follows the code_generator_registry pattern. It holds static templates for Express, FastAPI, Spring, nginx and Odoo-behind-nginx, each pinning the exact origin.
2. Data and wiring:
   - Add PrepareRequestUseCase to DI. It is currently only constructed inside SendRequestUseCase and GenerateCodeSnippetUseCase, so one registration line is needed in _registerRequestBuilder.
   - It gives the resolved URL, method and headers including auth and Content-Type, so Access-Control-Request-Headers matches what the app really sends. Only header names go on the wire, never the Authorization value.
   - Do not send the preflight through the shared locator<ApiClient>(). LenientCookieManager attaches jar cookies to the request and saves Set-Cookie from the response. A browser never does that on a preflight.
   - Instead create a CorsProbeClient: DioApiClient(cookieJar: CookieJar()), optionally wrapped in LoggingApiClient so the OPTIONS call shows in Console. Use ApiRequestOptions(followRedirects: false) and keep the user's timeout, TLS and proxy settings.
   - dart:io is not needed in this feature. Everything goes through ApiClient and Dio, and IOHttpClientAdapter accepts a custom Origin header. Gate with kIsWeb or a tiny canCheckCors getter, so no conditional import is required.
3. UI:
   - Add one tab 'Security' to ResponseToolsDialog (a new ToolTab line). It has a headers grade section on top and a 'Check CORS' section below, with an origin field defaulting to http://localhost:<port>, a 'with cookies/credentials' switch, and a fix-snippet selector with Copy.
   - Do not add two tabs. The dialog already has 9 tabs.
   - The palette item 'Check CORS for this request' opens a standalone CorsDoctorDialog (ToolDialog) that reuses the same pane. It reads ShellViewModel.selectedRequestId and RequestRepository.findById.
   - A standalone dialog is needed because ResponseToolsDialog opens only when there is a textual response that is not an example. A palette command must work without one.
4. Tests:
   - Unit tests for the analyzer matrix, grader and cookie splitter.
   - Add the dialog to dev_tools_dialogs_test (light/dark, desktop/phone, every tab visited) and a DI registration check in di_wiring_test.
   - A local HttpServer integration test for the preflight, with cookies not sent.

WEB AND MOBILE
- Android and iOS work. The feature is pure Dart plus Dio, and the ToolDialog is full-screen on phones.
- Web has two limits. The CORS check cannot run, because the browser forbids setting Origin and hides CORS failure details.
- Also on web, the header grader must be disabled too. The candidate only mentioned disabling CORS. A browser exposes only CORS-safelisted and exposed headers and never Set-Cookie, so grading would report false 'missing HSTS/nosniff' findings. Show an InfoBanner like TimingTab does, not failures. The web build is not in CI (build-and-release.yml mentions no web), so this is low priority.

CORRECTIONS TO THE PITCH
- Effort is M. The code is small, but the rule matrix, the context-aware grader, five server templates and the test matrix are the real work.
- A faithful simulation needs more than the candidate lists. Use a cookie-less client, no redirects, Sec-Fetch-* headers, the simple-request exception and the duplicate-header detection above.
- Step 2 of the real flow is that the actual response also needs Allow-Origin. A common cause is a 401/500 from a proxy or middleware that never added CORS headers. Re-sending the actual request with an Origin header is a real call with side effects. Do it automatically only for GET/HEAD. For writes, show an explicit 'send for real' button routed through ProductionGuard.checkSend. The preflight itself is OPTIONS and needs no guard.
- Value is bursty. 10 minutes x 1 per week is fair only for Flutter-web and Odoo-web developers. Most users will hit it a few times a quarter. The headers-grade half is lower value than the CORS half, so ship CORS first.
- Evidence: PostPilot already has an unreachableServerMessage web hint about CORS in lib/core/errors/unreachable_message.dart, which shows the pain is real. I could not re-verify the candidate's competitor claims this session, and I did not check whether Postman, Insomnia, Bruno or Hoppscotch offer a CORS tester. The candidate's evidence URLs were not re-fetched.

OPTIONAL FOLLOW-UP (S): a 'postpilot cors' CLI subcommand reusing the pure analyzers and DartIoSender. It would let CI catch a CORS regression.
- **Risks:** LEGAL/ToS/STORE: low. A single OPTIONS request and passive grading of a response the user already received are ordinary HTTP, the same as any request PostPilot sends. Nothing here conflicts with Play Store or App Store policy, since it only talks to user-chosen URLs. Keep it to at most 1-2 preflights per click. Do NOT add origin fuzzing or burst/rate-limit tests, which would turn it into a scanner against servers the user may not own. The candidate already drops rate-limit load testing, which is right. Add one line of UI text: only test servers you are allowed to test.

SECURITY RISKS TO HANDLE:
(1) Cookie leak and jar mutation. The shared ApiClient adds jar cookies to the OPTIONS call and saves Set-Cookie from its response. This was verified in lenient_cookie_manager.dart. Use a separate throwaway CookieJar, as designed above.
(2) Credential exposure. Only header names go in Access-Control-Request-Headers, never values. Console logging of the OPTIONS call therefore carries no secret.
(3) Bad fix snippets. Templates must never suggest '*' with credentials or blind origin reflection. Pin the exact origin and label localhost origins 'development only'.
(4) Writes. Never auto-resend a POST/PUT/DELETE with an Origin header. Keep that behind an explicit button and ProductionGuard.
(5) False findings. A headers grade that ignores context (CSP on JSON, HSTS on http, anything on web) will mislead and erode trust. The grader must be context-aware.
(6) Set-Cookie parsing is heuristic because the headers are joined into a single string. A cookie value containing ', name=' would mis-split. This is rare, so accept it and add tests.
(7) Fidelity. A dart:io OPTIONS call is a simulation of what Chrome does. A WAF or CDN that keys on User-Agent or Sec-Fetch headers may answer differently from a real browser. Say so in the UI.
(8) The Odoo 19 and nginx snippet details were not verified in this session. Check them against official Odoo and nginx docs before shipping. Include 'add_header ... always', handling OPTIONS with a 204, and proxy_hide_header to avoid duplicate Allow-Origin values.

OTHER: the Response tools dialog already has 9 tabs, so keep this to one new tab to avoid clutter. The tab bar is scrollable, so no layout risk. No database or schema migration is needed.
- **Strongest objection:** Weekly use is the weak point. A CORS incident happens when a project's web client is first wired to an API, so it is a once-or-twice-a-month event for a Flutter-web dev, not weekly. Many Flutter, backend and Odoo users never ship web at all. The honest rate is about 0.15 uses a week at roughly 10 minutes each, which is about 1.5 minutes a week. A security-header grade is also a once-per-project check, so it adds only about 0.5 minutes.

What the developer does today is already fast. Chrome DevTools prints the exact reason, such as "No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header" or "Request header field authorization is not allowed by Access-Control-Allow-Headers in preflight response". The Network tab and the Issues tab show the preflight itself. The "opaque XMLHttpRequest error" is only what Dart surfaces, and the console already holds the diagnosis. A one-line curl -i -X OPTIONS with Origin and Access-Control-Request-* headers shows the same thing, and PostPilot can already send a hand-built OPTIONS request. Plain-language copy-paste fixes for Express, FastAPI, Spring and nginx are a one-shot job for the existing "Ask AI" or a web search.

The slow part is not removed. The 10-30 minutes goes on editing server config and redeploying, and that step stays manual. The tool would also be a simulation, not the real thing. dart:io does not enforce CORS, so PostPilot only guesses what Chrome would do, and the guess can be wrong on credentials mode, extra headers, or servers that treat the real request differently from the preflight. The PostPilot web build cannot do it at all.

The security-header grading is a gimmick for this audience. securityheaders.com, curl -I, and the MDN Observatory cover it, and nobody grades the same API every week. It also adds a tab that a clean-UI product does not need.

The CORS-doctor part could be kept as a tiny add-on only if it is cheap. An "Odoo / Flutter web readiness" hint inside Odoo Studio (Odoo sends no CORS headers itself, so you need an nginx snippet) could be the one useful piece. Even that is a once-per-project saving, and the existing unreachable_message CORS hint plus Ask AI already cover most of it.
- **Platform notes:** Not possible in the PostPilot web build (browser forbids setting Origin and hides cross-origin results): disable there with a note. Works on desktop/mobile via dart:io as a simulation of what a browser would do. Skip burst/rate-limit load testing in v1; parse rate-limit headers only.

### 35. Performance smoke test with p50/p95/p99 thresholds
- **Score 0.8** | effort M | ~2 min/week saved | persona backend | lenses: qa-testing | skeptic votes 2/3
- **Problem today:** 'Is this endpoint still fast?' is answered by hitting Send a few times and reading the timing breakdown, or installing k6/hey/wrk and writing a command or script (8-10 min first time) and copying numbers into a PR. A single send hides tail latency and a +40% p95 regression goes unnoticed before release.
- **What PostPilot would do:** A 'Performance' mode in the runner for a request or collection: choose total requests and concurrency (capped), discard warm-up, run, and get p50/p90/p95/p99, max, error percent, requests per second and a histogram. 'Save as baseline' stores numbers and later runs show 'p95 +38% vs baseline'. Optional thresholds ('p95 < 500 ms', 'errors < 1%') make it pass/fail; the CLI exit code carries it. Positioned as a smoke test, with confirmation on production environments.
- **Where it lives:** Collection Runner dialog / request tab menu: a 'Functional | Performance' switch in run options. CLI: 'postpilot perf workspace.json --requests 200 --concurrency 10 --threshold p95<500'.
- **Partly exists already:** Only the building blocks exist; the feature itself does not. (1) Sequential repeat: lib/features/request_builder/domain/services/collection_runner_service.dart run() is an async* loop that awaits each send one at a time, with CollectionRunOptions (collection_run_options.dart) offering iterations up to 1000, a delay, stopOnFailure and CSV data rows. There is no concurrency (no Future.wait or Isolate in the runner; the only Future.wait worker pool is git_sync/domain/services/sync_engine.dart, _downloadConcurrency=8, for file downloads). (2) Timing stats: collection_run_report.dart CollectionRunSummary has only totalTime and averageTime, shown as 'Total time' and 'Avg time' in collections/presentation/widgets/collection_runner_dialog.dart (~line 374). There are no percentiles, histogram, max, error percent, requests per second or baseline anywhere in lib/ or bin/ (grep for percentile, p95, p99, histogram, baseline returned nothing relevant; compare_tab.dart only mentions 'keep a baseline' for diffing two responses). (3) Single-send threshold: a 'Response time below N ms' assertion exists (scripting/domain/entities/assertion_entity.dart, AssertionType.responseTimeBelowMs), but it checks one response, not a p95 across many. (4) Single-response timing: response_tools/presentation/widgets/timing_tab.dart shows total ms plus a DNS/TCP/TLS probe for one request. (5) CLI: lib/features/cli/cli_main.dart only has run, list and mcp. There is no perf subcommand, no --requests, --concurrency or --threshold option, and the reporters (cli/reporters.dart) emit per-request durationMs only, with no aggregates. So concurrency, percentile math, histogram, saved baseline with regression delta, p95/error thresholds and a perf CLI exit code are all new. Note for the build: the collection runner chains extractor variables forward, so a concurrent mode must be per-request or collection with extractors frozen/ignored, not a flag on the existing sequential run().
- **Competitors:** Verified by fetching the pages: Postman has performance tests with p90/p95/p99, avg and error % metrics, a pass/fail threshold (metric, condition, value), virtual users, load profiles (fixed, ramp-up, spike, peak), and `postman performance run` in the CLI, which exits 0 when thresholds hold and 1 when they fail. Local runs are on all plans, cloud runs need Solo/Team/Enterprise, and the CLI needs `postman login` (https://learning.postman.com/docs/tests-and-scripts/performance-testing/performance-test-configuration and https://learning.postman.com/latest-v-12/docs/postman-cli/postman-cli-run-performance-test). Grafana k6 Studio is a free open-source desktop GUI that builds k6 scripts visually, but per its docs the load run itself goes through the k6 CLI or Grafana Cloud, which shows demand for a GUI path (https://grafana.com/docs/k6/latest/k6-studio/introduction/). CLI tools k6, hey and wrk are the manual alternatives named in the candidate; I did not fetch them. I could not check Bruno, Insomnia or Hoppscotch because the web-search budget was used up, so I make no claim about them. The candidate's evidence URLs are real and match what it says. Verdict: it is genuinely new in PostPilot and has a real competitor. Its value is modest (about 12 min/week for one developer) and it is a smoke test, not a load tester, so the hard caps and the production-confirmation lock must stay.
- **Design in this codebase:** VERDICT: feasible without a rewrite; the architecture fits. Corrected effort is M, not S. The engine is S (2-3 days: worker pool plus percentile math), but the guards, baseline store, dialog with histogram, CLI subcommand with a shared-connection sender, and tests make it about 1-1.5 weeks.

DO NOT build it inside CollectionRunnerService or SendRequestUseCase. Two code traps (verified in lib/):
1. SendRequestUseCase.call() writes a History row per send (_historyRepository.record), and the ApiClient singleton is LoggingApiClient -> RequestConsoleLog (churns notifyListeners, cap 200). A 5,000-request run would flood the Drift History table and the console.
2. CollectionRunnerService yields a full CollectionRunResult per request and then runs scripts. That is the wrong shape for 5,000 samples. Also, collection_runner_dialog.dart is a raw Dialog with _Setup/_Results bound to CollectionRunResult (not yet on ToolDialog); a Functional|Performance switch there would bloat it. Keep the runner dialog untouched.

DESIGN
- New feature folder lib/features/performance/. domain/: PerfConfig (requests, concurrency, warm-up, optional rps cap, thresholds), PerfResult, Percentiles (pure Dart, nearest-rank p50/p90/p95/p99, histogram buckets), PerformanceRunnerService, PerfGuard. data/: PerfBaselineStore. presentation/: PerformanceViewModel (ChangeNotifier, registered as a factory in injector.dart like CollectionRunnerViewModel) and PerformanceDialog on the shared ToolDialog with the histogram widget.
- Request path: reuse PrepareRequestUseCase (it is the single path that resolves variables, auth and settings; today only built internally by SendRequestUseCase, so register it in DI or construct it inline). Prepare ONCE: it does ~4-5 Drift reads per call. Then get a fresh spec per sample WITHOUT touching the DB, because {{$guid}}, {{$timestamp}} and {{$randomInt}} (core/utils/dynamic_variables.dart) are evaluated at build time and JWT/SigV4 signing also embeds a timestamp. PreparedRequest already carries the layered resolver and effective auth, so add a small rebuild() on it (or pass RequestSpecBuilder + inherited auth) and call the pure-CPU builder per sample. Skip Digest and OAuth2 requests with a clear message (the CLI already does the same).
- Sending: a dedicated DioApiClient(cookieJar: CookieJar()) registered separately, NOT the logging singleton. No History rows, no console spam, and Set-Cookie from 5,000 responses does not mutate the user's real cookie jar. It reuses _ConfiguredAdapter (shared HttpClient, keep-alive, proxy and TLS settings) and ApiRequestOptions from settings. Timing is ApiHttpResponse.duration (time to last byte, includes Dio overhead); label it 'response time (full body)'. That is fine for relative regression on the same machine.
- Concurrency: a closed-loop pool of N async workers pulling from a shared counter, all in ONE isolate (50 workers and 5,000 requests do not need isolates; the isolate idea adds complexity for no gain). Cancel with the existing ApiCancelToken. Throttle UI notifyListeners to ~4/s, keep only an int list of durations plus error counts (5,000 ints is trivial). Wall-clock budget (e.g. 5 min) as a third cap.
- Error percent: transport error or non-2xx, and optionally the request's existing assertions (the CLI WorkspaceRunner.runRequest already runs them).
- Baseline: store a small JSON in SharedPreferences (already a dependency, works on web) keyed by the request's stable uid (entity_uids_dao) plus environment name. This avoids a Drift schema bump 4->5 with migration and build_runner. Thresholds live in the dialog and CLI flags in v1; putting them in workspace.json would need a BackupCodec change, so defer.
- Placement: (a) PaletteItem 'Performance smoke test' in palette_items.dart (one entry), (b) a 'Performance test' item in the request tab menu, (c) a case 'perf' in _handleAction of collections_sidebar.dart (pick one request). Single request first; per-request percentile table for a whole collection is a phase 2.
- CLI: lib/features/cli/perf_command.dart plus a shared-client sender. Existing sendWithDartIo builds and force-closes a new HttpClient per request, so every sample would include a cold TCP+TLS handshake. Add a sender that keeps one HttpClient alive and inject it through the existing CliSend typedef; WorkspaceRunner.runRequest then works as is. Add 'perf' to the command set and requests/concurrency/warmup/threshold/baseline/save-baseline to _Args (_valued). Exit codes follow the current contract: 0 pass, 1 threshold broken, 2 usage. Percentiles.dart must stay Flutter-free (like workspace_runner.dart) so the CLI imports it. ProductionDetector is pure Dart, so the CLI can refuse a prod-named --env unless --allow-production is passed.
- Platforms: the service is pure Dart over ApiClient, so it compiles on web with NO new conditional imports (DioApiClient already handles it through http_adapter_config). Desktop: full support. Android/iOS: dart:io works; default concurrency 5-10 and a note that radio and battery add noise. Web: clamp concurrency to 6 (HTTP/1.1 per-host browser limit; CORS applies) and show an info banner that browser numbers are not server numbers, or hide the feature. CLI is the dart:io-only part, and it already is.

HONEST NUMBER CAVEATS to put in the UI
- Show p99 only when samples >= ~500; with 200 samples p99 is 2 data points.
- 'p95 +38% vs baseline' needs a tolerance on both ratio and absolute ms (e.g. flag only if > 20% AND > 20 ms) or smoke runs will cry wolf from noise.
- A closed-loop generator under-reports tail latency when the server stalls (coordinated omission), and the client machine/network becomes the bottleneck at high rates. Call it a 'smoke test, not a load test'.
- Realistic value is about 5 min x 2 uses per week; the 6 min estimate is slightly generous, the real benefit is catching regressions.

EVIDENCE CHECK (fetched this session): Postman docs confirm p90/p95/p99, error %, requests/sec, 'Pass test if' thresholds, and CLI exit code 1 when a threshold fails; its CLI page documents 'postman performance run' with exit 0/1. The k6 Studio page confirms a free open-source desktop GUI that generates k6 scripts, i.e. demand for the GUI path. Both URLs are real and match the claim. Postman's docs state no restriction on production or third-party targets.
- **Risks:** 1. DoS-amplifier / legal (the main risk, MODERATE but mitigable). A one-click tool that fires up to 50 concurrent x 5,000 requests at any URL the user types can be pointed at a third party; the user (not PostPilot) carries the computer-misuse exposure, and PostPilot carries the reputational one. Mitigations to make mandatory: hard non-removable caps (50 concurrency, 5,000 requests, ~5 min budget, optional rps cap); a one-time per-host acknowledgement 'I own this server or have permission to test it' for any non-private host (localhost, 127.0.0.1, ::1, *.localhost, *.test, RFC1918, *.local are exempt), stored in SharedPreferences next to SafetyPrefs; no custom-cap bypass flag in the CLI except --allow-production style explicit flags. I could not verify any store-policy or provider load-testing policy text because the web-search budget was exhausted, so I make no claim about App Store / Play rules or AWS/GCP/Azure policies beyond: generic HTTP clients (Postman, Insomnia, k6 Studio) ship the same capability, and the caps above keep it at smoke-test scale. Re-check Google Play / Apple wording before a mobile store release.
2. Data-mutating methods (HIGH for Odoo users). Perf on POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE creates or changes N records (200 partners, 200 sale orders). Mitigation: default to GET/HEAD/OPTIONS only; write methods need an explicit checkbox that states the record count; reuse ProductionGuard.checkRun with writeCount = total requests (not 1) and wording specific to perf; in the CLI refuse prod-named environments without --allow-production.
3. History/console/cookie pollution (CERTAIN if built the naive way). Reusing SendRequestUseCase or the logging ApiClient writes thousands of History rows and cookie-jar mutations. Avoided by the dedicated DioApiClient plus PrepareRequestUseCase design above; add a test asserting zero history rows after a run.
4. Measurement validity (MEDIUM). Dio plus cookie manager plus stream read adds client-side latency; Dart is single-threaded so the client can queue at high rates; CLI per-request HttpClient would measure cold connections (fix with shared-client sender); small samples give noisy p95/p99. Mitigate with warm-up discard, p99 gating on sample count, tolerance-based baseline comparison, and clear labelling.
5. Platform (LOW-MEDIUM). Web: 6 connections per host and CORS make numbers meaningless, so clamp or hide. Mobile: battery/radio noise, keep small defaults. Mobile background suspension can stall a run; cancel cleanly on lifecycle pause.
6. Scope creep (LOW). Do not add workspace.json threshold storage, distributed runs, ramp profiles or scripting in v1: BackupCodec changes and a DB schema bump would turn M into L. Baselines are local (SharedPreferences) or a CLI-side JSON file the user can commit manually.
7. Dependency on a small refactor (LOW). PreparedRequest.rebuild() and DI registration of PrepareRequestUseCase touch the core send path; di_wiring_test.dart and send_request_usecase_test.dart / code_snippet_matches_send_test.dart already cover it and must stay green.
- **Strongest objection:** The claimed 12 min/week (6 min x 2 uses) is inflated on both numbers.

Uses per week. A typical backend, Flutter or Odoo developer runs a load or latency test a few times a month, not twice a week. Load testing is usually a release-time or incident-time activity owned by a few people, often run in CI. Flutter devs almost never do it. Odoo devs rarely get value from it: Odoo slowness comes from ORM and SQL behaviour, and a dev instance (often workers=0 or a single process) makes concurrency numbers meaningless. QA does real load work in JMeter or k6 pipelines, not a desktop client. Realistic use is about 0.3-0.5 per week.

Minutes per use. The one-liner alternative already exists and is fast: `hey -n 200 -c 10 -H "Authorization: Bearer ..." URL` or a 10-line k6 script. The 8-10 minute figure is a first-time cost for k6 and is paid once, then reused as a snippet. The real PostPilot gain is skipping the copy of the URL, headers, token and body into a command, about 2-4 minutes. That gain is real but small.

Honest estimate: about 0.5 uses x 4 min = about 2 min/week.

Trust problem. A "p95 +38% vs baseline" result measured from a laptop over Wi-Fi or VPN, against a shared dev or staging box, is noisy. Client-side CPU, network jitter and warm caches can swing it by that much. The feature invites false regressions and false comfort, and putting a baseline in a collection raises the risk of sync conflicts. The CLI pass/fail threshold would be flaky as a CI gate, and k6 already does that job properly.

Risk and cost. It needs concurrency, isolates, percentile math, a histogram view, baseline storage, prod-confirmation and caps, plus web limitations. The effort is not an S, and it adds a mode to the runner dialog, which works against the clean-UI goal.

Evidence. I could not get fresh demand data because the web search budget was used up (200/200). Postman's performance testing and k6 Studio show that the feature exists. They do not show weekly use by a typical developer. The code check confirms the runner only has sequential iterations (`CollectionRunOptions.iterations`, max 1000, plus `delay`) and no concurrency or percentile logic.

Cheaper variant worth keeping: show min/median/p95 in the existing runner report when iterations > 1. It is nearly free but still only about 1-2 min/week. It does not remove a painful or error-prone chore, so this candidate should not be a priority.
- **Platform notes:** Runner already supports iterations/delay; new work is concurrency, percentile math and a result view. Desktop/CLI via dart:io HttpClient and isolates. On web, ~6 connections per host and CORS make numbers meaningless, so limit or disable. Hard caps (e.g. 50 concurrent, 5,000 requests) and production confirmation so it cannot act as a DoS button; the client machine/network becomes the bottleneck at high rates.

## Rejected

- **Repro from text: turn a bug report, log line or traceback into a failing request** - value: The claimed 8 min per bug mostly isn't the typing PostPilot would automate. In practice the time goes on finding the real ids, body and account that trigger the bug, and free text almost never contains those. Most reproducible bugs already arrive as a browser network entry, which Copy as cURL plus the existing smart-paste handles in seconds. A server log line or an Odoo traceback has no request body or arguments, so the output would be a URL with {{placeholders}} that the dev still has to fill by hand. Most of the rest already exists. ai_request_dialog.dart already sends AiTasks.requestSystem (a description to one request, with {{baseUrl}}, {{token}} and Odoo json/2 handling), so a dev can paste the ticket text there today. The new part is a thin layer: host to environment mapping, auth to variables, PII placeholders, a default assertion and a Repro folder. That is roughly 1 to 2 minutes of tidy-up per bug, not 8. The red-now/green-after assertion (status 2xx, no error key) is weak. Many bugs are wrong values or empty lists returned with 200, and a model-guessed assertion would be misleading or need editing. It needs an Anthropic key and sends pasted logs to an LLM, and regex masking of PII in logs is imperfect, which makes cautious teams hesitant. Realistic use is about 2 times a week at about 2 minutes saved, so about 4 min/week. That is under the 10-minute bar, and it removes no painful or error-prone chore. I could not check demand evidence: the web search budget was exhausted. The Sentry, Chrome DevTools and Cybrosys links in the candidate show that bug triage and tracebacks exist, not that people want a replayable request generated from text. If kept, fold it into the existing Ask AI request dialog as an optional "mask + map env + add expectation" checkbox (about a day of work) rather than a separate palette feature. Or build deterministic parsers for HAR and network-entry JSON, Odoo log lines and call_kw paths. Those need no LLM and no key.

## Dropped by the deduper (duplicates or already present)

- Setup checklist for a new teammate or machine (missing local variables, hints, verify) - Lowest score of the pool (about 4 min x 0.5/week) and needs a schema addition for variable hints; one-off event per teammate. Cut by the 36-item cap.
- Postman import completeness (translate pm.* scripts, examples, descriptions, migration report) - One-off per team (about 0.1 uses/week per developer) and pattern-based translation is partial; low weekly time saved. Cut by the cap.
- Trash, undo and per-request revision history - Real safety value but rare use (about 0.7/week); lowest time-saved score of the larger items. Consider as a prerequisite if Find and replace or bulk edits ship. Cut by the cap.
- Docs pipeline: 'postpilot docs' CLI, richer static docs, publish to repo - About 8 min x 1/week; documentation generator already exists and the gain is publishing convenience only. Cut by the cap.
- Linked spec: auto-check OpenAPI URL, badge the collection and breaking-change report (merged from 'Linked spec' and 'API change report') - Low weekly savings (about 5 min x 2/week) and 'Update from OpenAPI' plus the Baseline and drift guard already cover most of the value. Cut by the cap.
- TLS and mTLS manager (client certificates per host, trust this cert, certificate inspector) - Narrow audience (only mTLS/self-signed servers), desktop and mobile only, passphrase handling risk; score about 4.8. Cut by the cap.
- OAuth/OIDC setup wizard (issuer discovery, loopback login, device-code flow) - One-time setup per IdP, desktop-only loopback listener; score about 4.8. The recurring pain (expiry) is covered by Self-renewing auth. Cut by the cap.
- Odoo data mover: paged export and dry-run import through load() (merged from two finders) - Large effort (L) for about 1-1.5 uses/week; score about 5. Export-only slice is small but overlaps Fetch all pages plus table/CSV. Cut by the cap; revisit after the bulk runner.
- Odoo 17/18 connection (legacy JSON-RPC transport) - Finder admits it saves no time by itself and owner's stated target is Odoo 19 JSON-2; value is zero for pure-19 teams. Cut by the cap (score about 6 after a realistic estimate).
- Connection doctor: TLS, cleartext and 'phone cannot reach it' diagnosis - Lowest frequency of the Flutter ideas (about 0.75/week), dart:io exposes no SAN or chain so it needs an X.509 parser; score about 6. Cut by the cap.
- Sync to project: regenerate Dart code without losing hand edits, CLI gen --check, --dart-define env export (merged from two finders) - Real pain but desktop/CLI only, needs a new text three-way merge, about 10 min x 1.5/week (score about 6). Cut by the cap; good follow-up to the Dart test generator.
- Auth break-tests: generate JWT/token tamper variants with expected 401/403 - Mostly a subset of the OpenAPI test generator's Auth family (no/bad token); JWT tamper variants only add alg=none/expired/tampered claims. Score about 6.4. Cut by the cap.
- Collection changelog: field-level 'what changed' after Pull, PR comment and release notes - About 4 min x 4/week at M effort (score about 6.4) and the push preview/diff already exists. Cut by the cap.
- Move, copy, multi-select and drag-and-drop in the sidebar with bulk actions - Good ergonomics but a UI-interaction feature rather than automation of developer work; score about 7.2 and single lens. Cut by the cap.
- Webhook inbox with one-click public URL (cloudflared/ssh tunnel, replay, HMAC verify) - Desktop only, depends on external tunnel binaries, public-endpoint security burden; score about 7.2 and single lens. Cut by the cap.
- API layer v2: Retrofit output, typed error model and 401 refresh in generated Dart - Per-project saving (about 20 min x 1/week) and generated-code style choice, narrower than other Flutter items; single lens, score about 8. Cut by the cap.
- Schema-aware test-data seeder (Odoo models and OpenAPI bodies) - Single lens, depends on the bulk runner and cleanup ledger shipping first (score about 9, same tier as kept items but lower strategic fit). Cut by the cap.
- Resume where I left off: restore tabs, pins, expanded tree and last environment - Cheap (S) but only about 1.5 min x 6/week and it is a UX nicety, not automation of developer work. Tie-broken out by the cap.
